4. **Keep polling on a schedule:** no public path, but drift waits up to a day (two hours after a daily scan),
   and every scan starts a run.

#### Consequences

- Good: drift reaches the agent minutes after the scan. Clean scans start nothing. The receiver holds no
  GitHub credential and can call nothing but Temporal, so a leaked secret can at most start drift runs for
  in-scope repositories, which the agent re-reads from GitHub.
- Bad: a new public hostname with Access bypassed. It is limited to one path, signed deliveries, and a
  1 MB body limit. The webhook secret lives in OpenBao `kv/wardens/github-webhook` and in the App's settings,
  and must be rotated in both places.
- The receiver's repository pattern (platform-wardens `framework/config/webhooks.yaml`) must stay in step with
  the agent's scope (`config/scope.yaml`).

#### Related

- ADR 0003 (Cloudflare tunnel and Access), ADR 0058 (public access through the tunnel), ADR 0069 (`wardens`
  cluster), ADR 0074 (operator console)
- platform-wardens decision 0015 (event bus, deferred: the webhook adapter stays until it is built)
- ADR 0082 (amends this: a second App on the same path, one secret per App, `workflow_run` rules)

## ADR-0076: The stack-author agent: a chat in the operator console that ends in a draft pull request, one Temporal workflow per conversation
{: #adr-0076 }

<span class="adr-status adr-status-proposed">Proposed</span> <span class="adr-meta text-muted">2026-11-04 · `homelab-gitops`, `infra-local`, `platform-wardens`, `terragrunt-shared-module`</span>

#### Context

Adding a resource to a stack means finding the right repository and stack, reading a module's README and writing
`resources.yaml` by hand. The operator console already signs people in and starts agents. platform-wardens decision 0026
has the agent's design; this record covers how it runs on the homelab.

#### Decision

- **Where:** a new `stack-author` worker on the `wardens` cluster from `charts/wardens-agent`, and an **Author** tab in the
  existing console (no new route or sign-in; the console's Keycloak client already covers it).
- **Conversation:** each one is a Temporal workflow on the agent's queue, started by the console on the `console-chat`
  channel. It's closed after 30 idle minutes, and the channel's deadline is 4 hours. Only the person who started it can
  read it or send to it.
- **Identity:** its own GitHub App, `cloudutsuk-wardens-stack-author` (ADR 0071), with contents and pull requests write
  and actions read, installed on `terragrunt-shared-module` and the infra-* repositories only. Its tokens are narrowed
  further in code: the write token doesn't cover the shared module.
- **Model:** the local model through LiteLLM with its own virtual key. Another provider is a setting.
- **Memory:** short-term is the workflow's state. Long-term is a small volume (`memory.enabled` in the chart, 0.1.4)
  holding advisory notes per person that expire after 90 days. Losing it costs a few suggestions, so it isn't backed up.

#### Considered options

- **Keep the conversation in the console's process:** simpler, but a console restart loses it and it leaves no run record.
- **A separate chat app with its own route and sign-in:** another thing to run for what is one more tab.
- **Reuse the drift agent's GitHub App:** rejected, one App per agent (ADR 0071), and that App's scope is every repository.

#### Consequences

- Merging the platform-wardens change before this one fails its image job at the digest commit, because
  `apps/stack-author/values.yaml` must exist. Merge this one first. Until the first image the Application shows a
  template error (`image.digest is required`), the same as every new agent.
- Conversations stay in Temporal's history for the namespace's retention (30 days).
- GitHub Free has no branch rules, so the "pull request only" guard is the agent's own code, as for the drift agent
  (platform-wardens decision 0013's amendment). The owner merges by hand.

## ADR-0077: The agents' state lives in a second Postgres in wardens, wardens-state-db, separate from Temporal's
{: #adr-0077 }

<span class="adr-status adr-status-accepted">Accepted</span> <span class="adr-meta text-muted">2026-11-04 · `homelab-gitops`, `platform-wardens`, `infra-local`</span>

#### Context

The platform-wardens framework defines a store interface for each kind of state an agent leaves behind,
and ships only local versions of them (files or memory):

| State | Framework interface | On the homelab today |
|---|---|---|
| Run records (one per run, category 22) | `RecordSink` | Files under `RUN_RECORD_DIR`, an `emptyDir`: deleted whenever the worker pod is replaced, which every image digest bump does |
| Artifacts (findings, proposed actions) | artifact store | Files, or only inside Temporal's history |
| Dedupe claims (cooldown per trigger) | `DedupeStore` | In process memory: lost on restart, not shared between replicas |
| Kill switch | `KillSwitchStore` | A ConfigMap per agent from Git: engaging it is a commit, an Argo CD sync and a kubelet refresh, more than the 60 s the manifests promise (`maxStopSeconds`) |
| Audit log (category 11) | `AuditLog` | A local JSON-lines file with a hash chain |
| Long-term memory (category 21) | — | A 100 MiB volume per agent (`memory.enabled`, used by stack-author), not backed up, one per pod |

ADR 0070 put Temporal on `wardens-db`, one database per cluster as for `ai-db` and `platform-db`. Putting the
agents' state there too was considered and rejected (option 2): Temporal's chart can't drop another role's
database (its `temporal` role isn't a superuser, `createDatabase: false`, its schema jobs only set up and
update), but the instance is what gets reset. On a homelab the usual fix for a broken Temporal is to wipe
and recreate its database or volume, and a restore of `wardens-db` to recover Temporal would roll the
agents' records back with it. Temporal's history is infrastructure that can be thrown away; the agents'
records and audit log are data that must outlive it.

ADR 0073 moved backups to Garage on the DGX Spark, kept 7 days, with no object lock and no off-site copy.

#### Decision

- **A second Postgres in `wardens`: `wardens-state-db`** (namespace `wardens-state-db`, `apps/wardens-state-db`),
  built like `wardens-db`: Postgres 17 pinned by digest, the restricted security context, data on a hostPath
  volume `/srv/homelab/pg/wardens-state-db` with `Retain`, the dump hook annotations of ADR 0039 (inert until
  Velero runs in `wardens`, ADR 0073), passwords from OpenBao `kv/wardens/wardens-state-db` seeded by
  infra-local `host/wardens/configure.sh`. This is the exception to "one database per cluster" in ADR 0070.
- **One database, `wardens`, and one role per kind of writer**, created by the init script:
  - `wardens_owner` owns the schema and is used only by the schema job;
  - `wardens_agent` (every agent worker) reads and writes run records, artifacts, dedupe claims and memory,
    reads the kill switch, and may only **insert and read** audit events;
  - `wardens_console` (the operator console) has the agent's rights plus engaging and releasing kill switches;
  - `wardens_reader` (dashboards, the audit anchor) reads only.

  No role but `postgres` may update or delete an audit event.
- **The framework owns the schema.** platform-wardens ships numbered SQL migrations with `wardens.state`, and a
  plain Job in `apps/wardens-state-db` (named after the schema version, like Temporal's schema job; not an
  Argo CD hook) applies them as `wardens_owner` with the console image, which carries the framework.
- **Only Temporal-labelled namespaces connect:** a NetworkPolicy lets in pods from namespaces labelled
  `homelab/wardens-agent=true` (every agent and the console) on 5432, and the schema job's pods in the database's
  own namespace, nothing else.
- **Each store, with how long it is kept:**
  - Run records and artifacts: rows of schema-checked JSON, kept 180 days, 30 days for `confidential` data.
    Large inputs (a plan file) are stored as a reference and a hash, never copied.
  - Dedupe claims: a unique fingerprint and an expiry; a claim is one `INSERT … ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING`.
  - Kill switch: one row per scope (`all` or an agent), engaged and released by operators from the console and
    audited. An agent is stopped when **either** its row or its Git ConfigMap says so, and when the database
    can't be read (fail closed). The ConfigMap stays as the break-glass that needs no database.
  - Audit: insert-only, with the existing hash chain, kept 1 year. Once a day a schedule reads the chain head
    and the day's count as `wardens_reader` and sends them to `mon` as a log line and a metric. `mon` is
    another cluster with other credentials, so rewriting the chain afterwards no longer matches what `mon`
    recorded.
  - Long-term memory: none unless a manifest approves it (category 21); then rows with provenance,
    classification, expiry and a reviewed flag. stack-author keeps its volume until it moves to the table.
  - A daily schedule deletes what has expired.
- **Agents select the store by configuration** (`WARDENS_STATE_DSN` from the role's Secret); without it the
  framework keeps its local stores, as in tests and local runs.

#### Considered options

1. **Keep files and ConfigMaps:** no new service, but run records are lost on every deploy, the kill switch is
   slower than the manifests promise, and nothing is shared between replicas.
2. **A `wardens` database on `wardens-db`:** one Postgres fewer and safe from Temporal's chart (roles), but it
   shares Temporal's resets, restores, upgrades and load.
3. **Object storage (Garage) for records and artifacts:** cheap for blobs, but these are small records that
   need queries by correlation id, agent and outcome, and it gives no atomic claim for dedupe.
4. **Redis (`redis-cache` on `ai`) for dedupe:** a cache that may evict, in another cluster; dedupe is a
   correctness guard.
5. **Loki as the audit log:** retention-limited and not tamper-evident. It keeps the daily anchor instead.
6. **A second Postgres, `wardens-state-db`** (chosen).

#### Consequences

- Good: run records survive redeploys; Temporal can be reset or restored without touching the agents' records
  or audit log.
- Good: an operator can stop an agent from the console within seconds, audited.
- Good: one implementation of the framework's stores, which on Azure points at PostgreSQL Flexible Server.
- Bad: another Postgres to run (about 128 MiB to 512 MiB), another hostPath to move in a migration, another
  dump to back up.
- Bad: if `wardens-state-db` is down, every agent stops (fail closed). Intended, and alerted.
- Bad: until Velero runs in `wardens` (ADR 0073), this database isn't backed up. Backups are kept 7 days with
  no off-site copy, so the audit log's history beyond that lives only on the PC's disk.

#### Related

- ADR 0035 (data ownership), ADR 0039 and 0073 (backups), ADR 0069 (the `wardens` cluster), ADR 0070 (Temporal
  on `wardens-db`), ADR 0072 (deployment standard), ADR 0074 (the operator console)
- platform-wardens framework categories 06, 11, 21, 22 and 23

#### Implementation (2026-11-05)

- Running: `apps/wardens-state-db`, the schema job (`wardens-state-schema-0001`, migration 0001), and run records
  from every agent (`state.enabled` in `charts/wardens-agent` 0.1.6). Alerts: `WardensStateDbDown` and
  `WardensRunRecordNotStored` (`docs/runbooks/wardens-alerts.md`).
- One choice beyond the decision above: if the database can't be reached when a run ends, the agent writes the run
  record to its local file instead of failing the run, and `WardensRunRecordNotStored` fires.
- Built in the framework, not used yet: dedupe, the kill switch, the audit log (a trigger makes it insert-only for
  the owner too), memory, artifacts, and the retention and anchor commands (`wardens.state.maintenance`). Their
  migrations need a new schema job, and the retention and anchor jobs need CronJobs.
- Backups, until Velero runs in `wardens` (ADR 0073): a nightly `pg_dump` of `wardens-db` and `wardens-state-db`
  onto the DGX Spark's disk (infra-local `host/backup/wardens-dumps.sh`, 7 nights, each checked with `pg_restore`),
  alerted by `WardensDumpTooOld`. Unencrypted, in a 0700 folder; a restore into a throwaway Postgres was tested.

## ADR-0078: Per-app Keycloak groups (admin, editor, viewer) as the only place access is assigned
{: #adr-0078 }

<span class="adr-status adr-status-accepted">Accepted</span> <span class="adr-meta text-muted">2026-11-04 · `infra-local`, `homelab-gitops`, `self-service-portal`, `job-hunter-agent`, `shorts-studio`</span>

#### Context

ADR 0028 made Keycloak the single OIDC issuer with two groups, `platform-admins` and `viewers`, mapped to
roles in each tool. That worked for one person. Adding people showed the gaps:

- There was no tier between read-only and owning everything, and no way to give someone a role in one app
  without giving it in all of them.
- Several apps admit whoever the issuer signs in (Hermes, LiteLLM) or hold keys, cluster-admin or a
  signed-in session (Job Hunter, Shorts Studio, Headlamp, Homarr, Temporal UI, the Wardens console). One
  shared `browser platform-admins only` flow guarded all of them with the single `platform-admin` role.
- Cloudflare Access let only `platform-admins` through on every public UI, so a viewer couldn't reach
  Grafana or Argo CD even though both would have given them a read-only role.

The owner wants to assign a person's access in one place and have every app honour it.

#### Decision

- **Groups live in Keycloak, realm `homelab`.** Not in Entra. Entra stays the identity provider (who you
  are, MFA); Keycloak holds authorization. Entra groups would reach Keycloak only as object IDs in the
  Entra token, needing one identity-provider mapper per group, applied only at the next broker sign-in,
  and they would leave the Keycloak-issued `groups` claim that every app already reads as a second copy.
- **Names.** `platform-admins`, `platform-editors`, `platform-viewers` mean that role in every app (the
  all-apps tier). `<app>-admin`, `<app>-editor`, `<app>-viewer` mean that role in one app, for: `grafana`,
  `argocd`, `portal`, `kubernetes`, `openbao`, `velero`, `hermes`, `litellm`, `job-hunter`,
  `shorts-studio`, `headlamp`, `homarr`, `temporal`, `wardens-console`. Groups are flat, each with a
  `description` attribute; the `groups` claim carries short names (`full.path: false`).
  `host/keycloak/configure-realm.sh` creates all 45 and assigns people with `--grant <group> <email>` and
  `--revoke <group> <email>`.
- **Each app maps the `groups` claim to its own roles**, honouring both the all-apps group and its own,
  most privileged first. A person's effective role in an app is the highest of the two.
- **Roles mean the same everywhere.** Viewer reads; editor operates and changes through audited paths (git,
  Argo CD sync, dashboards, lab deploys); admin owns the app. Editors and viewers never read secrets.
- **Kubernetes:** `kubernetes-admin` and `platform-admins` get `cluster-admin`; editors and viewers get
  `view`. The built-in `edit` ClusterRole reads and writes Secrets, which here are copies of OpenBao paths,
  and Argo CD's self-heal reverts hand edits anyway.
- **Per-app gates replace the shared one.** Each gated client has its own realm role `<client>-access`,
  mapped to exactly the groups in the script's `APP_ACCESS` table, and its own browser flow
  `browser <client> gate` that refuses everyone else. The table is the truth: the script removes the role
  from groups that are no longer listed. The old flow and its `platform-admin` role stay in the realm,
  unbound, until removed by hand.
- **Apps without a safe read-only role stay admin-only.** All three groups exist for every app, but an
  app's `<app>-editor` and `<app>-viewer` are added to its gate (and to its Access policy) only once the
  app maps them to a role that is safe for them. Until then they are refused by Keycloak.
- **Cloudflare Access** gains an `allow: groups` mode that lists the groups an application admits. Apps
  with their own roles admit every group of the app; admin-only apps admit `platform-admins` and
  `<app>-admin`, the same as their gate.
- **As built (2026-11-04):** gates admit all six groups for Shorts Studio and Homarr (both map the groups
  to their own permissions); LiteLLM admits `platform-admins` and its three `litellm-*` groups only (free
  SSO stops at five users), and its fixed `litellm_role: proxy_admin` claim is gone; Hermes, Job Hunter,
  Headlamp, Temporal UI and the Wardens console stay admin-only. Velero UI and OpenBao need no gate (their
  own RBAC and bound claims decide).
- **The legacy `viewers` group is retired.** It was empty; it is no longer created or mapped and is left in
  the realm until deleted by hand.
- **Planned (2026-11-04):** the groups, gates and the `APP_ACCESS` table move to a Terragrunt stack (ADR 0079).
  The access model above doesn't change. Membership stays out of git: `--grant` and `--revoke` remain commands,
  and the realm stays the only place that records who is in a group.

#### Considered options

1. **Entra security groups synced into Keycloak**: assignment in the Entra admin center, but 40+ mappers
   to keep in step, object IDs instead of names, and changes only at the next broker sign-in.
2. **Keycloak client roles per app** instead of groups: closer to each app's own roles, but most apps here
   read a `groups` claim, and assigning a client role is less obvious in the console than a group.
3. **Global groups only** (`platform-admins/editors/viewers`): simple, but no way to give someone a role in
   one app.
4. **Per-app groups in Keycloak, plus the all-apps tier** (chosen).

#### Consequences

- Good: one console (or one command) assigns a person's role in any app; no app keeps its own users.
- Good: a gated app can be opened to editors or viewers with a one-line table edit once the app is ready.
- Bad: every app's mapping now lists two groups per role, and a new app needs its three groups, its
  mapping and, if gated, a table entry and an Access policy.
- Bad: tokens and app sessions keep the old groups until the next sign-in, so a revoke isn't immediate.

#### Related

- [ADR 0028](#adr-0028)
- [ADR 0058](#adr-0058)
- [ADR 0068](#adr-0068)
- [ADR 0079](#adr-0079)

## ADR-0079: Keycloak's configuration moves from configure-realm.sh to a Terragrunt stack; group membership stays out of git
{: #adr-0079 }

<span class="adr-status adr-status-proposed">Proposed</span> <span class="adr-meta text-muted">2026-11-04 · `terragrunt_shared_module`, `infra-local`, `homelab-gitops`</span>

#### Context

Keycloak's realm `homelab` (ADR 0028) is configured by one imperative script,
`infra-local/host/keycloak/configure-realm.sh`, 460 lines of `curl` and `jq` run by hand. It has grown with every
app, and ADR 0078 made it the place access is assigned. The script owns:

| Object | Count | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| Realm | 1 | Lifespans, brute-force protection, no registration |
| Groups | 45 | `platform-{admins,editors,viewers}` and `<app>-{admin,editor,viewer}` for 14 apps |
| Clients with a `groups` mapper | 15 | 3 public with PKCE, 12 confidential |
| Realm roles `<client>-access` and their group mappings | 8 roles, 17 groups | The `APP_ACCESS` table |
| Browser flows `browser <client> gate` | 8 | Each is 1 flow, 3 subflows, 5 executions and 3 configs |
| Identity provider `entra` | 1 | Plus its redirector config on the built-in `browser` flow |

That is about 200 objects, counted from the script rather than the live realm. The problems:

- A change shows no plan: the effect of a table edit is known only after the script has run against the only
  issuer of the platform.
- Nothing detects a change made in the admin console, and the script can't remove what it no longer lists, apart
  from the `APP_ACCESS` role mappings. ADR 0078's legacy objects are "left until removed by hand" for this reason.
- It is the one piece of platform configuration with no pull request, plan or apply record, while the clusters,
  Azure, GitHub and Cloudflare have them (ADR 0006, ADR 0060, ADR 0072).
- Flow steps need hand-written priority fixes, because the API gives every new step priority 0.

The [`keycloak/keycloak` Terraform provider](https://registry.terraform.io/providers/keycloak/keycloak/latest/docs)
(5.10.0 when checked on 2026-11-04) covers every object above. Three constraints shape how it can be used:

- Secrets live only in OpenBao (ADR 0023), but Terraform state is in Azure Blob and plans are CI artifacts.
- Only Keycloak stores email addresses (ADR 0035), and this repo's decisions are published on a public blog.
- A recreated group loses its members, and Keycloak is what every sign-in depends on.

#### Decision

- **Modules go in the shared module.** `terragrunt_shared_module` gains a `providers/keycloak` family, the same
  shape as `providers/grafana`, because Keycloak is a provider against a running application and not a cloud. One
  module per resource type, as everywhere else. The list is in that repo's `pending_resource_types.md`.
- **The stack goes in `infra-local`**, under `environments/k3d/homelab/`, with state in the existing backend. CI
  plans on the pull request and applies on `main` on the `homelab-iac` runner, which is on the network that reaches
  the admin API. `ubuntu-latest` can't: `/admin` is not served on the public name (ADR 0058).
- **Everything the script creates moves to the stack**: the realm, groups, clients, mappers, roles, role mappings,
  gate flows and the Entra provider. `APP_ACCESS` becomes YAML in `resources.yaml`, still the single table to edit.
  `keycloak_group_roles` with `exhaustive = true` keeps its rule that a group not listed loses the role.
- **Group membership is not IaC.** `keycloak_group_memberships` would put people's addresses in git, which breaks
  ADR 0035, and it needs users who exist only after their first broker sign-in. Who is in a group stays operational
  data in Keycloak. `configure-realm.sh` shrinks to `--grant` and `--revoke` and is renamed to say so. The stack
  manages the groups and never their members.
- **Secrets stay out of state.** Clients and the identity provider use the provider's write-only arguments,
  `client_secret_wo` with `client_secret_wo_version`, never `client_secret`. They need Terraform 1.11 or later, so
  the module's stacks require it (`root.hcl` allows 1.9.8 today). `kv/core/keycloak` stays the source: its key names
  don't change, so the `host/*/configure.sh` scripts that copy secrets to each cluster's path keep working. A new
  client's secret is generated into OpenBao first, as today, and the plan fails if the key is missing. Rotation is
  a new value in OpenBao plus a bump of the version.
- **The provider signs in as its own client**, using the client credentials grant: a confidential service-account
  client in the `master` realm with the `admin` role, because this stack owns the realm itself and has to be able to
  recreate it. Its secret is in OpenBao under `kv/ci/`. A short bootstrap script creates it once with the bootstrap
  admin, which goes back to being emergency-only. `url` is the public issuer and `admin_url` the internal name.
- **Import, never recreate.** Existing objects enter state through import, so UUIDs, memberships and secrets are
  unchanged. The realm's module sets `prevent_destroy`.
- **Migration order:**
  1. Export the live realm and diff it against the script, so settings changed in the console are decided on rather
     than lost.
  2. Add the provider and modules to the shared module.
  3. Write the stack and import every object.
  4. Merge only when the plan shows no create, replace or destroy. The expected difference is the
     `client_secret_wo_version` values, and any other in-place change is explained in the pull request.
  5. Apply, then sign in to every client and check its gate still refuses someone outside its groups.
  6. Cut the script down to membership, and update the docs that name it.
  7. Delete the legacy objects from ADR 0028 and ADR 0078 (the `viewers` group, the `browser platform-admins only`
     flow and the `platform-admin` role) through the stack or by hand.
- **Drift is reported, not silently overwritten.** The generated workflow's daily drift scan (ADR 0075) covers this
  stack like any other, so a change made in the console shows up as drift.
- **To confirm while building:** that the `homelab-iac` runner resolves both names and trusts their certificates,
  how the write-only values reach Terragrunt at plan and apply (a `TF_VAR_` environment read from OpenBao, or an
  ephemeral resource from the `vault` provider), and the manual path when `platform`, and so the runner, is down
  during a rebuild of `core` (ADR 0025).

#### Considered options

1. **Keep the script**: no work and no new failure modes, but none of the problems above go away, and it grows with
   each app.
2. **Keycloak realm import (`--import-realm`) or `keycloak-config-cli`**: one realm file in git and no state, but no
   plan to review and no drift detection, it is a second way of working next to every other stack, and the file would
   need secret substitution.
3. **Terragrunt with membership in the stack too**: the whole realm in one place, but emails in git (ADR 0035), a
   sign-in needed before the plan can apply, and an authoritative resource that removes anyone added any other way.
4. **A separate `infra-keycloak` repo**, as with `infra-cloudflare` (ADR 0060): cleaner ownership, but one more repo
   for a single stack, and it would still need the homelab runner.
5. **Terragrunt in `infra-local`, membership left out** (chosen).

#### Consequences

- Good: every access or client change is a pull request with a plan, and console changes are detected.
- Good: removing an app or a legacy object removes it from the realm, without a hand-written cleanup.
- Good: flows are declared with explicit priorities, so the script's priority fixes go away.
- Good: secrets stay in OpenBao, and only a version number reaches state.
- Bad: a bad apply can break sign-in everywhere, so the plan is what protects it. Break-glass (ADR 0028) stays.
- Bad: importing about 200 objects by UUID is a one-off job, and needs a script to read the IDs from the admin API.
- Bad: two places remain: the stack for what exists, and `--grant` / `--revoke` for who belongs to it.
- Bad: a master-realm admin client is a powerful credential that CI can read. It is scoped by the `kv/ci/` policy and
  the runner, not by Keycloak.
- Bad: onboarding an app changes: a stack entry and a pull request, not a script edit and a run. The app READMEs that
  name the script need updating at step 6.

#### Related

- [ADR 0023](#adr-0023)
- [ADR 0025](#adr-0025)
- [ADR 0028](#adr-0028)
- [ADR 0035](#adr-0035)
- [ADR 0058](#adr-0058)
- [ADR 0060](#adr-0060)
- [ADR 0072](#adr-0072)
- [ADR 0075](#adr-0075)
- [ADR 0078](#adr-0078)

## ADR-0080: The operator console gets a public name, console.cloudutsuk.com, behind Cloudflare Access
{: #adr-0080 }

<span class="adr-status adr-status-proposed">Proposed</span> <span class="adr-meta text-muted">2026-11-05 · `homelab-gitops`, `infra-local`</span>

#### Context

ADR 0074 kept the operator console internal: `console.wardens.home.cloudutsuk.com`, no tunnel route, no Access
application, reachable only on the LAN or the tailnet. The owner now wants to open it from devices without
Tailscale, the same way as the other browser UIs (ADR 0058).

The console is more sensitive than most of those UIs. It starts agent runs on the real workers, which spend the
agents' LiteLLM budget and can open draft pull requests, and it has no read-only mode.

`charts/wardens-web` took one `host`, and its oauth2-proxy sidecar had one fixed `--redirect-url`. With a fixed URL, a
sign-in started on the other name comes back to a host that doesn't hold its cookies, and fails.

#### Decision

1. **The console also answers at `console.cloudutsuk.com`**, a flat public name (ADR 0058). The internal name stays,
   for use on the tailnet without Access.
2. **Two sign-ins, both limited to the same groups.** An Access application allows `platform-admins` and
   `wardens-console-admin`, the groups of the client's Keycloak gate and of oauth2-proxy's `--allowed-group`. The
   owner's one-time PIN is on every Access application (ADR 0058), but it opens only Access: the console still needs
   a Keycloak sign-in in an allowed group.
3. **`charts/wardens-web` gains an optional `publicHost`**: a second Ingress rule and certificate. When it is set,
   oauth2-proxy has no fixed redirect URL, so each name uses its own callback, and `--whitelist-domain` names both.
   Both callbacks are on the Keycloak client. Other users of the chart are unchanged.
4. **The tunnel forwards the name to the wardens load balancer** (172.30.0.6), as for `hooks.cloudutsuk.com`.
5. **`hooks.cloudutsuk.com` is unchanged**: it is a separate Ingress and still serves only `/github`.

#### Considered options

1. **Stay internal, with Tailscale on every device** (ADR 0074): the smallest attack surface, but the owner wants
   access from devices without it.
2. **Access only, without oauth2-proxy on the public name**: Access's headers would have to replace the sidecar's, and
   the app would need a second way to learn the requester.
3. **Replace the internal name with the public one** (a single redirect URL, as ADR 0058 does for apps with one base
   URL): a Cloudflare or tunnel outage would then also lock the console out on the tailnet.
4. **Both names, Access and Keycloak on the public one** (chosen).

#### Consequences

- Good: the console opens from any device after Access and Keycloak, with the requester recorded as before.
- Good: the internal name keeps working when Cloudflare or the tunnel is down.
- Bad: a tool that starts agent runs now has a public name. A wrong Access policy would leave Keycloak's gate as the
  only check, and the reverse; the agents' guardrails, ceilings and kill switch still apply (ADR 0074).
- Bad: anyone who signs in through Access uses a Zero Trust seat (ADR 0058).

#### Related

- ADR 0058 (public access through the tunnel and Access), ADR 0074 (the console, amended here), ADR 0075 (the tunnel
  to the wardens load balancer), ADR 0078 (per-app groups)

## ADR-0081: Chat model: Qwen3.6-35B-A3B (FP8, Apache 2.0) replaces Qwen3.5-35B-A3B
{: #adr-0081 }

<span class="adr-status adr-status-accepted">Accepted</span> <span class="adr-meta text-muted">2026-11-05 · `homelab-gitops`, `infra-local`, `sakharams.github.io`</span>

#### Context

The chat model has been Qwen3.5-35B-A3B in FP8 since 2026-10-16 (ADR 0049), in `vllm-chat` on the DGX Spark's
`gpu` cluster, in a 0.36 slice (about 44 GB; its measured `MemAvailable` drop is 54 GB, ADR 0063). Its callers
are agents: Hermes, job-hunter, Shorts Studio and the platform-wardens agents terraform-drift and stack-author.

Qwen has released newer open models since (checked 2026-11-05):

- **Qwen3.6-35B-A3B** (April 2026, Apache 2.0): the same architecture (`Qwen3_5MoeForConditionalGeneration`),
  35B parameters with 3B active, the same linear-attention layout and context, and an official FP8 build.
- **Qwen3.6-27B and Qwen3.8-27B** (April and August 2026): dense. Every token reads all 27B parameters, so on
  the GB10's memory bandwidth they decode several times slower than a 3B-active MoE.
- **Qwen3.8-Flash-Next** (August 2026, `qwen-community-1.0` licence): 125B total, 6B active, plus a 51B n-gram
  table, about 180B parameters on disk. FP8 needs about 180 GB; even 4-bit needs about 100-125 GB, over the
  88 GB the model arbiter can admit above its 15 GB floor, and its new attention needs a newer vLLM than the
  26.07 image (newer images need a newer driver than the Spark's 580).
- Qwen3.7 is hosted only; Qwen4 hasn't shipped.

Qwen's card for Qwen3.6-35B-A3B compares it with Qwen3.5-35B-A3B (thinking mode, vendor-measured):

| Benchmark | Qwen3.5-35B-A3B | Qwen3.6-35B-A3B |
|---|---|---|
| SWE-bench Verified | 70.0 | 73.4 |
| SWE-bench Pro | 44.6 | 49.5 |
| Terminal-Bench 2.0 | 40.5 | 51.5 |
| SkillsBench | 4.4 | 28.7 |
| MCPMark | 27.0 | 37.0 |
| TAU3-Bench | 68.9 | 67.2 |
| Tool Decathlon | 28.7 | 26.9 |
| MMLU-Pro | 85.3 | 85.2 |
| GPQA | 84.2 | 86.0 |
| LiveCodeBench v6 | 74.6 | 80.4 |

#### Decision

- **Model:** Qwen/Qwen3.6-35B-A3B-FP8, Apache 2.0, pinned to revision `95a723d0` (2026-04-24).
- **Same app and settings:** `apps/vllm-chat` keeps the image (`nvcr.io/nvidia/vllm:26.07`, vLLM 0.24; the card
  asks for 0.19 or later), the 0.36 slice, 128k context, `--language-model-only`, the `qwen3_coder` tool parser,
  the `qwen3` reasoning parser, and the two workarounds (`VLLM_USE_DEEP_GEMM=0`,
  `VLLM_ENFORCE_STRICT_TOOL_CALLING=0`).
- **Tested first:** the weights were downloaded to the shared models folder and served on the GB10 with the
  deployment's exact image, flags and environment before the change
  (2026-11-05, a container on the Spark, nothing else loaded):
  - weights 33.4 GiB, loaded in 214 s from a cold page cache, then 97 s to profile, compile and warm up;
    ready 5 min 50 s after start;
  - KV cache 265,216 tokens, 2.02 requests at the full 131,072;
  - `MemAvailable` fell from 106.3 to 57.9 GB (48.4 GB, under the arbiter's 54 GB for `vllm-chat`);
  - a plain answer with thinking off, thinking split into `reasoning_content` (17 × 23 = 391), an `auto` tool
    call and a streamed tool call with thinking on all came back correctly;
  - 512 tokens in 11.0 s, about 47 tokens a second including prefill.
- **Gateway name:** the model is published as `qwen3.6-35b-a3b`. The old name `qwen3.5-35b-a3b` stays in LiteLLM
  as an alias to the same backend, so no caller breaks while it moves:
  1. this change: the model and both names;
  2. every caller's virtual key gets `qwen3.6-35b-a3b` (infra-local `host/*/configure.sh`, Hermes' by hand);
  3. the callers switch to the new name;
  4. the alias and the old name in the keys are removed.
- **Fallback:** Qwen3.5-35B-A3B's weights stay on the `models` claim; going back is a revert of the model commit.

#### Considered options

1. **Keep Qwen3.5-35B-A3B:** works, but Qwen3.6 is better at the agent work every caller does, at no cost in
   memory or speed.
2. **Qwen3.6-35B-A3B in FP8** (chosen): a drop-in upgrade.
3. **Qwen3.8-27B (dense):** the best scores among models that fit, but a dense 27B decodes several times slower
   on the GB10, and the agents make many calls.
4. **Qwen3.8-Flash-Next:** the strongest open Qwen, but it doesn't fit in the memory the arbiter can admit, would
   evict every other model, and needs a newer vLLM than the Spark's driver allows. Revisit with a smaller Flash
   variant or a driver upgrade.
5. **Keep the name `qwen3.5-35b-a3b` for the new model:** no caller changes, but logs, metrics and costs would
   name the wrong model.
6. **A model-agnostic name (`chat`):** future swaps without caller changes; a bigger change to every caller and
   key, left for later.

#### Consequences

- Good: better agentic coding and tool use for every caller, with the same memory, speed, licence and settings.
- Good: the alias keeps every caller working during the move.
- Bad: another 37.5 GB on the `models` claim while both versions are kept.
- Bad: the model arbiter's `size_gb` for `vllm-chat` (54) was measured on Qwen3.5; re-measure after the switch.
- Bad: the vendor's benchmarks aren't ours; Phase 5 step 7's evaluation runs should confirm them.

#### Related

- ADR 0010 (vLLM on the GB10), ADR 0022 (the gateway), ADR 0049 (superseded), ADR 0051 (scale to zero),
  ADR 0061 (the Spark as model host), ADR 0063 (the model arbiter)

## ADR-0082: The CI/CD triage agent gets failed runs through its own GitHub App on the existing webhook path, one secret per App
{: #adr-0082 }

<span class="adr-status adr-status-proposed">Proposed</span> <span class="adr-meta text-muted">2026-11-05 · `homelab-gitops`, `infra-local`, `platform-wardens`</span>

#### Context

platform-wardens decisions 0027 and 0028 add a `cicd-triage` agent on `wardens`. It needs to know when a GitHub Actions run fails, in any org repository, within minutes.

ADR 0075 built the entry point for drift: `wardens.runtime.github_webhook` at `https://hooks.cloudutsuk.com/github`, with one secret, reading `workflow_job` events from the drift agent's App. ADR 0071 gives each agent its own App, so triage can't reuse the drift App's identity. ADR 0075 rejected `workflow_run` for drift because that event can't say whether drift was found. For a failed run, it's the right signal: it fires once, after the run ends, with the conclusion in the payload. In a sample of failed runs, 12% had no failed job at all, and those send no `workflow_job` event.

The approval wait for a fix can last 24 hours, and the channel `github-webhook` caps autonomy at level 1 and the deadline at 120 minutes.

#### Decision

- **A new App, `cloudutsuk-wardens-cicd-triage`** (`infra-local/host/github/manifests/wardens-cicd-triage.json`), subscribed to `workflow_run`, sending to the same URL. Permissions grow by release: reads first, then `pull_requests: write`, then `contents: write`, then `workflows: write`. Write tokens are narrowed when minted. It never gets `administration`, `secrets` or `actions: write`.
- **One receiver, one secret per App.** The receiver tries each App's secret from `kv/wardens/github-webhook`. Same path, same tunnel route, same Access bypass, so there's **no new public surface**. It still checks the HMAC before parsing, and still holds no GitHub credential.
- **Rules generalised in `framework/config/webhooks.yaml`:** keys `event`, `workflowIdPrefix` and `channel`, and `workflowName` becomes optional. Drift's rules, behaviour and workflow ids don't change.
- **A rule for failed runs:** conclusions `failure`, `timed_out` and `startup_failure`; every org repository except the public blog; not `cicd-fix/` branches, whose runs the agent already watches. Workflow id `incident-cicd-triage-<owner>-<repo>-<run>-<attempt>`, so a rerun's failure gets its own analysis and a redelivery starts nothing. Start delay 120 seconds, so that runs on the same commit finish.
- **A new channel, `ci-failure`:** deadline 1,800 minutes, and `maxAutonomyLevel` following the agent's grant at each release.
- **A fallback, as in ADR 0075:** a daily schedule starts any default-branch failure that a lost delivery missed.
- **Worker first, rule last.** The rule ships disabled until the worker runs on `wardens`.

#### Considered options

1. **Same path, one secret per App (chosen):** nothing new is exposed, and each App's secret rotates on its own.
2. **Same path, one shared secret:** the smallest change, but rotating it means changing both Apps at once, and a leak affects both.
3. **A second tunnel path:** clean separation, but more public surface for no real gain, since the signature is the authentication.
4. **Add `workflow_run` to the drift App:** no new App, but the drift App holds `contents: write`, and ADR 0071 gives each agent its own identity.
5. **Poll the Actions API on a schedule:** no webhook, but it misses the 5-minute target and uses API quota on every repository.

#### Consequences

- Good: failed runs reach the agent in minutes, and the receiver stays small and credential-free.
- Good: a leaked secret can at most start read-only runs that re-check GitHub.
- Bad: the receiver now handles every org repository's run events, about 3 deliveries a run. It answers most with a cheap 200.
- Bad: a third secret to keep in OpenBao and an App's settings, and the receiver's repository pattern has to stay in step with the agent's scope.
- To check on first deliveries: `workflow_run` payload size against the receiver's 1 MB limit.

#### Related

- ADR 0071 (per-agent identities), ADR 0075 (the webhook entry point, amended here), ADR 0083 (`reusable-workflows`)
- platform-wardens decisions 0027 and 0028

## ADR-0083: build-image.yaml and its gate move to reusable-workflows, the central repository for application pipelines; IaC workflows stay in the shared module
{: #adr-0083 }

<span class="adr-status adr-status-proposed">Proposed</span> <span class="adr-meta text-muted">2026-11-05 · `homelab-gitops`, `reusable-workflows`, `infra-local`, `homelab-services`, `self-service-portal`, `job-hunter-agent`, `shorts-studio`, `platform-wardens`</span>

#### Context

Three repositories hold shared workflows:

| What runs | Lives in | Callers |
|---|---|---|
| Image build and security gates | `homelab-gitops/.github/workflows/build-image.yaml` | 14 call sites in 6 repositories |
| IaC | `terragrunt-shared-module/.github/workflows` | All 6 `infra-*` repositories |
| Language CI, Sonar, JFrog, Azure deploy | `reusable-workflows` | One external caller |

`reusable-workflows` was meant to be the standard, but its content is behind: 2 of 135 third-party actions pinned to a SHA, 1 of 52 jobs with a timeout, and 10 files with runner labels this org doesn't have. Application pipelines should have one home, which the standards catalog and the `cicd-triage` agent (platform-wardens decisions 0027 and 0028) can both rely on.

`build-image.yaml` can't simply be moved, because OpenBao's role `main` is bound to it. `infra-local/host/openbao/configure-ci.sh` requires this claim:

```text
job_workflow_ref = cloudutsukpilot/homelab-gitops/.github/workflows/build-image.yaml@refs/heads/main
```

`job_workflow_ref` names the reusable workflow running the job, not the repository calling it. Role `main` grants Zot push, Cosign signing, the `homelab-gitops` writer App and the BuildKit certificate. So this one file is the only thing in the org that can publish a signed image. If it moved with no other change, every main build would fail at its credentials step, while pull request builds kept passing.

#### Decision

- **`reusable-workflows` becomes the central repository for application pipelines.** `build-image.yaml`, `.github/actions/security-tools` and `policies/conftest` (with `policies-check.yml`) move there. Whether `ci/tools` moves depends on what the runner images need from it, checked during the move.
- **The digest commit and the Argo CD refresh stay pointed at `homelab-gitops`,** which is still the deploy repository.
- **IaC stays out of this migration.** The workflows in `terragrunt-shared-module/.github`, its `template/`, and the `infra-*` pipelines don't move. The older Terragrunt copies in `reusable-workflows` are left alone and aren't accepted references.
- **Fix `reusable-workflows`' application workflows first:** runner labels and JFrog hosts become inputs, `continue-on-error` comes off gates, leftovers go, then SHA pins, `permissions` and timeouts. `sonarqube-ci.yaml` stays compatible, because the shared module calls it at `@main`.
- **Order, so that nothing breaks at any step:**
  1. Copy the files. The old ones keep working.
  2. Role `main` accepts both paths.
  3. Prove it with `hello-world` in `homelab-services`, through a full main build: push, sign, digest commit, Argo sync, Kyverno admission.
  4. Move the other callers, one repository at a time, each proven by a real main build.
  5. Remove the old path from role `main`, and replace the old file with a stub that fails with a pointer.

#### Considered options

1. **Move them, with both claims allowed during the move (chosen):** one home, and each step can be reversed.
2. **Keep `build-image.yaml` in `homelab-gitops`:** costs nothing today, but leaves two central repositories for application pipelines, and path-specific rules in the agent.
3. **Move the shared module's workflows too:** a single central repository, but its renderer, compatibility test and consumers are a project of their own. Left out by the owner's choice.
4. **Bind role `main` to the caller, not the called file:** no move needed for OpenBao, but any repository's workflow could add a step and use the Cosign key. Rejected.

#### Consequences

- Good: application pipelines have one home, which the catalog can name, and the agent has one write scope for them.
- Good: Kyverno isn't affected. It checks signatures against `cosign.pub`, a key and not a path.
- Bad: whoever can push to `reusable-workflows` `main` can publish signed images, and GitHub Free can't protect that branch. Today only one account has write on both repositories, so this is no worse than now. Giving the fix approver write on it, so they can merge fixes, widens this on purpose.
- Bad: a mistake shows only after merge, since pull request builds never reach role `main`. That's why step 3 proves it with a low-risk repository.
- Bad: the agent can write to the repository holding the signing path (decision 0028). A branch can't reach role `main`. The first commit of a fix there needs approval; later ones don't.
- IaC remains a second central location.
- Older ADRs (0008, 0032, 0033, 0034, 0043) stay as history. This one supersedes only where they name the path.

#### Related

- ADR 0008 (CI build path), ADR 0033 (signing and admission), ADR 0043 (build path), ADR 0082 (triage webhook)
- platform-wardens decisions 0027 and 0028

## ADR-0084: Alerts and chat reach the wardens agents rules first, then a classifier, then a person; Slack keeps every alert
{: #adr-0084 }

<span class="adr-status adr-status-proposed">Proposed</span> <span class="adr-meta text-muted">2026-11-05 · `homelab-gitops`, `infra-local`, `platform-wardens`</span>

#### Context

Alertmanager on `mon` sends alerts only to Slack, so no agent sees them (Phase 6 step 1). The operator console's chat
talks to one agent, stack-author, and more chat agents are coming, starting with one that authors CI/CD pipelines.
platform-wardens decision 0029 settles how requests find an agent: alerts are structured and their labels are known in
advance, so rules route them; free text has nothing to match, so a classifier on the local model proposes an agent. This
ADR records how that runs on the homelab.

#### Decision

- **Alerts:** Alertmanager sends every warning and critical group to the platform-wardens alert receiver as well as to
  Slack, through a route with `continue: true`. Slack's behaviour doesn't change, so an alert no agent handles is
  still seen, and escalating one costs nothing extra.
- **Receiver:** `wardens-alerts` in the `wardens-console` namespace (`apps/wardens-console/templates/alerts.yaml`), the
  console's image with `python -m wardens.runtime.alert_webhook`. Internal only, at
  `https://alerts.wardens.home.cloudutsuk.com/alert`. It checks a bearer token before reading the body, because
  Alertmanager can't sign requests, and reads only allowlisted labels, never annotations. It can reach Temporal and
  nothing else.
- **Router worker:** `wardens-router` in the same namespace (`templates/router.yaml`), same image, task queue
  `wardens-router`. It runs the classifier, and the incident workflow for routed alerts, so an alert doesn't depend on
  one agent's worker. It has its own LiteLLM key and reaches HTTPS for LiteLLM only through the namespace's policy.
- **First target:** a minimal `incident-triage` worker (`apps/incident-triage`), which records the alert as a finding and
  a Slack message. Only the named test alert `WardensRoutingTest` routes to it until it can diagnose real ones.
- **Secrets:** infra-local `host/wardens/configure.sh` generates the token once in `kv/wardens/alert-webhook`, copies it
  to `kv/mon/alertmanager` `wardens_token`, and creates the router's LiteLLM key in `kv/wardens/wardens-router`.

#### Considered options

1. **Every alert through the classifier:** rejected in decision 0029. Alerts need no judgment to route, annotations can
   carry attacker-influenced text, and the model may be down during the very incidents that matter.
2. **Move alerts from Slack to the agents:** rejected. Until an agent diagnoses alerts, Slack is how people see them.
3. **The receiver on the public tunnel, like `hooks.cloudutsuk.com`:** not needed. Alertmanager is on `mon`, which
   already reaches `*.wardens.home.cloudutsuk.com`.
4. **The classifier inside the console or the receiver:** rejected. A local model's turn outlasts any request.

#### Consequences

- Good: a known alert reaches its agent with no model call; LiteLLM being down doesn't stop it.
- Good: a new chat agent is a manifest change in platform-wardens, with no change here.
- Bad: two more Deployments in `wardens-console`, and a second copy of the token in `kv/mon/alertmanager`.
- Bad: until the classifier's threshold is measured (platform-wardens `evals/routing`), an alert no rule knows only
  escalates.

#### Related

- platform-wardens decision 0029; ADR 0069 (the `wardens` cluster), 0074 (the console), 0075 (the GitHub webhook).

## ADR-0085: Stack Author offers choices and only the clouds that can create the resource; a weekly job refreshes its resource list through its own GitHub App
{: #adr-0085 }

<span class="adr-status adr-status-proposed">Proposed</span> <span class="adr-meta text-muted">2026-11-05 · `platform-wardens`, `infra-local`, `homelab-gitops`, `terragrunt-shared-module`</span>

#### Context

The stack-author agent (ADR 0076) answered "I need to create a storage account" with a plain-text question that listed
all six clouds. GitHub, Cloudflare and k3d have no storage module. The model had only `list_targets`, which knows
clouds but not modules. Its module search covered one cloud at a time and matched words in folder names, so it found
Azure's `azurerm_storage_account` but not AWS's `aws_s3_bucket`. The person also had to type a cloud's name back.

What each cloud can create changes whenever the shared module gains a module or an infra repository gains a cloud. A
list kept only by hand goes stale. Keeping it fresh means reading the private shared module and the `infra-*`
repositories on a schedule, and proposing the change as a pull request. No CI identity could do that: OpenBao's role
`pr` only gets Zot pull, and role `main` is limited to `build-image.yaml` (ADR 0043).

#### Decision

1. **Questions carry choices.** The model's question may include up to eight choices, each with a label, a value and
   a hint. The console shows them as numbered buttons. A reply that is a choice's number or label reaches the model
   as that choice, resolved in workflow code.
2. **Only clouds that can create the resource are offered.** A read tool, `find_resource`, matches the request
   against hand-written resource kinds in `config/targets.yaml` (what people ask for, and the module for it in each
   cloud) and against module names. It lists only clouds that have such a module in the shared module now. Ranking:
   a cloud the request names, then modules whose name contains a word of the request, then use in that cloud's
   stacks. When a question's choices are clouds, code cuts them to that list, whether or not the model called the
   tool.
3. **A weekly job refreshes the generated part of `targets.yaml`.** It runs on Mondays and by hand, in
   platform-wardens' `stack-author-targets.yaml`. It reads three things: each cloud's modules in the shared module,
   each `infra-*` repository's `environments/<cloud>/`, and which modules each stack's `resources.yaml` uses. It
   rewrites only the block below a marker: per-cloud module usage counts, and targets for clouds that no hand-written
   target lists. When the block changes, it opens or updates a pull request on `stack-author/targets-refresh`. The
   notes name modules no kind covers. A person merges, and the agent's build deploys the change.
4. **The job has its own GitHub App, `cloudutsuk-stack-author-refresh`.** It's installed on platform-wardens,
   terragrunt-shared-module and the `infra-*` repositories. Its key is in OpenBao `kv/ci/wardens-targets-refresh`, read
   through role `github-actions/wardens-targets-refresh`. The role is bound like `main`: the org's ID, `refs/heads/main`,
   a self-hosted runner, and `job_workflow_ref` set to that workflow file. Each run mints two tokens: contents read
   for reading, and contents and pull requests write on platform-wardens only for the pull request. Both are revoked
   at the end.

#### Considered options

1. **Let the model work out which clouds fit**, from module names or its own knowledge: not chosen. A small local
   model listed every cloud in practice, and nothing would stop it doing so again.
2. **Rebuild the list in the worker at runtime**: not chosen. It needs no new credentials, but nothing is reviewed,
   and new modules and clouds never surface as a change for a person to see.
3. **Use the stack-author App for the job**: not chosen. That App is the agent's identity, narrowed to the infra
   repositories. Giving it write on platform-wardens would let the agent change its own configuration.
4. **A deploy key or token stored as a GitHub secret**: not chosen. It breaks the rule that CI credentials come only
   from OpenBao (ADR 0043). A pull request opened with `GITHUB_TOKEN` also doesn't start CI.

#### Consequences

- Good: a request gets only clouds that can create the resource, as buttons, and "1" or a click answers. The cut is
  in code, so a weak model can't undo it.
- Good: a new module or cloud reaches the agent through a reviewed pull request, with notes on what the resource kinds
  don't cover.
- Bad: one more App and OpenBao role to keep. The App's installation-wide permissions include write on the infra
  repositories, though each run's tokens are narrower.
- Bad: resource kinds are hand-written. A kind that's missing or too narrow offers too few clouds until someone edits
  it. The weekly notes list candidates.

#### Related

- ADR 0043 (CI credentials from OpenBao, roles bound to one workflow), ADR 0019 (self-hosted runners), ADR 0071 (agent GitHub Apps), ADR 0076 (the stack-author agent)
- platform-wardens decision 0026; roadmap Phase 6 step 21a

## ADR-0086: Waiting CI jobs named through a read-only GitHub App, homelab runners raised to 6, and ARC alerts
{: #adr-0086 }

<span class="adr-status adr-status-proposed">Proposed</span> <span class="adr-meta text-muted">2026-11-05 · `homelab-gitops`, `homelab-services`, `infra-local`</span>

#### Context

The Platform / GitHub Actions runners dashboard (Phase 1 step 1e) counts the jobs waiting for a runner but
can't say which they are. ARC's listener only knows how many jobs GitHub assigned to its scale set: it has
no job names, and a job GitHub hasn't assigned yet, because the scale set is full, doesn't show at all. Only
GitHub's API names them (a run's jobs with status `queued`).

The first day of metrics (2026-11-05) showed the `homelab` scale set, the org's build and test runners, at
its maximum of 3 for 36 minutes, with up to 2 more jobs waiting and a p95 queue wait of 2 minutes. On the same
day the busiest runner peaked at 1.3 GiB and all runners together at 5.3 GiB, on a homelab PC with 32 cores
and about 50 GB free. Nothing alerted on a full scale set or a missing listener: `ScrapeTargetDown` only
sees a listener pod that exists and fails.

#### Decision

1. **A small exporter names the waiting jobs.** `ci-queue-exporter` (homelab-services, deployed to `platform`)
   polls every 30 seconds (from each poll's start): the repositories its App is installed on, each repository's queued and in-progress
   runs, and the jobs of those runs only. It publishes `github_actions_job_waiting_seconds`, one series per
   waiting job (repository, workflow, job, status, runner labels, run and link), valued at how long it has
   waited. Requests are conditional (ETag); GitHub doesn't count a `304`, so a quiet poll costs almost nothing.
2. **Its credential is a new GitHub App with Actions and metadata read only**, `cloudutsuk-ci-queue-reader`,
   created with infra-local `host/github/create-app.py` (manifest `ci-queue-reader.json`), key in OpenBao
   `kv/platform/ci-queue-reader`. External Secrets' `GithubAccessToken` generator turns the key into an
   installation token in the exporter's namespace and refreshes it within the hour, so the pod never holds
   the key.
3. **The `homelab` scale set may run 6 runners**, as `homelab-iac` does. Only each runner's 512Mi request is
   reserved; the 12Gi limit stays for the SBOM steps of large images.
4. **Alerts:** `ARCListenerDown` (a scale set with no listener for 10 minutes) and `ARCScaleSetAtCapacity`
   (more jobs assigned than runners for 20 minutes) in `alerts/platform/arc-runners.yaml`; with the exporter,
   `GitHubActionsJobWaitingTooLong` (a job for our runners waiting over 15 minutes, by name) and
   `CIQueueExporterDown`. Added 2026-11-10: `ARCControllerReconcileErrors` (10 or more failed reconciles in an
   hour, per controller; the normal rate was about 15 a day, all retried). Added 2026-11-06: `ARCJobsAssignedNoBusyRunners` (jobs assigned and no busy runner
   for 15 minutes) and an *Offline runners* stat on the dashboard (registered minus busy minus idle, with
   registered capped at the controller's running runners, because the listener's counts freeze between
   GitHub's messages). On
   2026-11-05 every runner lost GitHub's Actions service for hours while its pod stayed Running; the
   dashboard read 0 running, 0 busy, 0 idle, which looked like a quiet queue, and no alert fired because
   assigned jobs never exceeded the runner limit.
5. **Queued is not assigned.** GitHub hands a scale set at most its maxRunners jobs; the rest stay queued in
   GitHub, where only the exporter sees them. So the dashboard counts *Runners starting* (assigned, not yet
   running, from ARC) apart from *Jobs queued in GitHub* (every queued job, from the exporter).

#### Considered options

1. **Reuse the ARC App** (`cloudutsuk-arc-runners`): rejected. It holds org self-hosted-runner and repository
   administration write; a read-only exporter shouldn't carry that key.
2. **A personal access token**: rejected. Tied to a person, and a fine-grained token can't be limited to
   Actions read across the org as cleanly as an App installation.
3. **A `workflow_job` webhook** (event-driven, no polling): rejected for now. It needs a public endpoint and a
   webhook secret for a dashboard table; polling with ETags is cheap at 33 repositories.
4. **An off-the-shelf GitHub Actions exporter**: rejected. The ones we found report runs and durations from
   webhooks, not the jobs waiting now.
5. **Leave `homelab` at 3**: rejected. Two-minute waits at the 95th percentile on a quiet day, with the PC mostly
   idle.

#### Consequences

- Good: the dashboard's **Jobs waiting and running now** table names each waiting job with a link, and a job stuck longer
  than 15 minutes alerts by name, which catches a runner label nothing serves.
  Since 2026-11-10 the exporter also publishes `github_actions_job_running_seconds` (one series per running
  job, with its runner), so the same table shows what the runners are busy with.
- Good: the exporter can't change anything in GitHub, and its token expires within the hour.
- Bad: the generator's spec holds the App ID and installation ID, so they're committed after the App is
  created and installed (not secrets).
- Bad: up to 12 runners across both scale sets at once. If memory gets tight, the PC's node limit and the
  runner requests decide, and `ARCScaleSetAtCapacity` plus the dashboard's memory panels show it.

#### Related

- ADR 0019 (self-hosted runners, private repos only), ADR 0040 (the `homelab-iac` scale set), ADR 0026 (monitoring collection)
- ADR 0027 (alert rules for the Mimir ruler)

## ADR-0087: LiteLLM's admin UI signs in through Keycloak only; the admin + master key login is off
{: #adr-0087 }

<span class="adr-status adr-status-accepted">Accepted</span> <span class="adr-meta text-muted">2026-11-06 · `homelab-gitops`</span>

#### Context

LiteLLM's `/ui` takes two sign-ins: Keycloak SSO (ADR 0078 maps groups to roles) and a username and password
from the environment (`UI_USERNAME`/`UI_PASSWORD`, or `admin` and the master key when `UI_PASSWORD` is unset).
The second path makes anyone holding one shared static secret a `proxy_admin`, with no per-person audit, and
the UI is reachable from the internet behind Cloudflare Access (ADR 0058). LiteLLM v1.103.1 warns about it in
the UI and offers `general_settings.disable_env_credential_login: true` to turn it off.

#### Decision

Set `disable_env_credential_login: true` in `apps/litellm/config.yaml`. Admins sign in through Keycloak
(`platform-admins`, `litellm-admin` → `proxy_admin`). The master key keeps working as an API bearer for
creating and revoking virtual keys (ADR 0029). Break-glass, when Keycloak is down or LiteLLM's free SSO has
stopped (more than five users): set the setting to `false` in git, sync, sign in with `admin` and the master
key, then set it back.

#### Considered options

1. **Turn it off (chosen)**: one sign-in path, per-person, behind Keycloak's gate and brute-force detection.
2. **Keep it, with a separate `UI_PASSWORD`**: still a shared static admin secret, just a different one.
3. **Keep it as is**: the documented fallback, but the weakest way into the gateway's admin.

#### Consequences

- Good: every UI admin is a named Keycloak user; the master key is no longer a UI password.
- Bad: no UI access while Keycloak is down or SSO has stopped until the break-glass change is synced.

#### Related

- ADR 0022, ADR 0029, ADR 0058, ADR 0078

## ADR-0088: The wardens ingress bus runs on the Event Hubs emulator in wardens, with mcr.microsoft.com mirrored through Zot
{: #adr-0088 }

<span class="adr-status adr-status-proposed">Proposed</span> <span class="adr-meta text-muted">2026-11-06 · `homelab-gitops`, `infra-local`, `platform-wardens`</span>

#### Context

platform-wardens decision 0030 puts an event bus at the agents' front door. The Alertmanager and GitHub webhooks
publish to an Event Hubs hub and answer at once, and one consumer routes events to Temporal. The bus code is built and
merged (platform-wardens #73, #76, #77); its plan is `platform-wardens/docs/plans/event-bus-ingress.md`, roadmap
Phase 6 step 33. The owner chose Microsoft's **Event Hubs emulator** for the homelab rather than an Azure namespace
(2026-11-06).

The emulator and Azurite (its metadata and the consumer's checkpoints) are published only on `mcr.microsoft.com`.
Every cluster pulls public images through Zot (ADR 0034), and Kyverno's `restrict-image-registries` admits only Zot
and the registries Zot mirrors (ADR 0033). MCR was neither.

Both images run under `pod-security-restricted` and a read-only root. This was checked with Docker on 2026-11-06:
the emulator as user 1654 and Azurite as user 1000, all capabilities dropped, no new privileges, with writable
emptyDirs for `/tmp`, the emulator's `/home/app` and Azurite's data folder. The emulator came up, created its hubs,
and kept its metadata in Azurite.

#### Decision

1. **`mcr.microsoft.com` becomes the sixth registry Zot mirrors:**
   - Zot's on-demand sync gets it under `mirror/mcr.microsoft.com/` (infra-local `helm/core/zot`);
   - k3s `registries.yaml` rewrites its pulls to that path (infra-local `host/k3s/registries.yaml`);
   - Kyverno's `mirroredRegistries` admits it.

   ADR 0034's mirror list is amended accordingly. A cluster picks up `registries.yaml` only after
   `k3d cluster stop/start`. `wardens` is restarted first, and the other clusters when convenient. Until then their
   pulls from MCR go straight to Microsoft, which Kyverno allows because it checks the image name, not the path.
2. **The ingress bus runs in `wardens`, namespace `wardens-console`, as one pod with two containers:** the Event Hubs
   emulator and Azurite.
   - **No persistent volume:** the emulator keeps nothing across a restart anyway, and resetting Azurite with it keeps
     the consumer's checkpoints in step with the hub. The consumer also detects a reset (platform-wardens 0030).
   - A NetworkPolicy lets only the relays and the consumer reach AMQP (5672) and Azurite's blob port (10000).
   - The connection strings are the emulator's and Azurite's published development defaults. They still come from
     OpenBao (`kv/wardens/eventhubs`), like every other credential.
   - Built in plan step 4b (`apps/wardens-console` `templates/eventhubs.yaml` and `templates/ingress.yaml`):
     - the two containers share the pod's network namespace, so the emulator reaches Azurite on `localhost`. The
       bus has no network egress at all (the emulator was checked to start with none);
     - the consumer `wardens-ingress` is the console's image running `wardens.runtime.ingress`, one replica to
       start (up to one per partition), with `/healthz` for the kubelet;
     - `alerts.mode` and `hooks.mode` (`direct` or `relay`) switch each webhook; `relay` adds the hub's
       connection string and an egress rule to the bus. Both stay `direct` until the cut-over (step 4c), and
       `direct` is the rollback.
     - Checked before merging (2026-11-06): platform-wardens' emulator integration tests pass against the pod's
       shape in Docker (shared `localhost`, the users above, read-only root, emptyDirs); a restart of only the
       emulator container recreates the hubs with a new `created_at`, which the consumer treats as a reset; and a
       server-side dry run on `wardens` passes Kyverno's enforced policies (while a writable-root variant is
       refused).

#### Considered options

1. **Mirror MCR through Zot (chosen):** consistent with ADR 0033/0034. Pulls are cached and scanned like any other
   public image, and the next Microsoft image needs no exception. The cost is a `k3d cluster stop/start` per cluster.
2. **Copy the two images into Zot by digest:** no restarts, but a manual copy on every version bump, and a second
   way of bringing public images in.
3. **A Kyverno PolicyException for MCR in `wardens`:** no restart and no copy, but the pull bypasses Zot, which is
   what ADR 0033 exists to prevent.
4. **An Azure Event Hubs namespace instead of the emulator:** durable and supported, but it costs money and needs the
   internet for every alert. Left as the later target in platform-wardens 0030.

#### Consequences

- Good: every cluster can use Microsoft images the normal way, and the bus runs inside `wardens` next to the
  consumer and relays, with no cloud cost.
- Bad: the emulator is not for production. An emulator restart loses events accepted but not yet read (the
  consumer reads within seconds; an alert fires on emulator restarts, platform-wardens plan phase 5), and Microsoft
  doesn't support it.
- Bad: one more upstream registry in Zot's sync, which needs the internet for a first pull like the others.

#### Related

- [0033](#adr-0033) image rules, [0034](#adr-0034) Zot (mirror list amended here)
- platform-wardens decision 0030 and its plan `docs/plans/event-bus-ingress.md`

## ADR-0089: LiteLLM prices the local models at what the nearest Azure model costs
{: #adr-0089 }

<span class="adr-status adr-status-proposed">Proposed</span> <span class="adr-meta text-muted">2026-11-06 · `homelab-gitops`</span>

#### Context

Every model behind LiteLLM runs on the homelab's own GPU, so LiteLLM knows no price for any of them: the
`hosted_vllm/*` and `openai/flux.1-schnell` / `openai/ltx-2.5` names aren't in its cost map. The UI's Costs
column shows `-`, every key's spend stays at $0, and a key budget (ADR 0029) never trips. The only cost on a
dashboard is the electricity estimate on AI / Model performance and usage (GPU energy × the `kwh_price`
variable), which is per token for all of vLLM, not per call or per key.

Phase 4 (Inventory + cost) wants to show what the homelab saves against a cloud, and the agents (Paperclip,
the wardens) need budgets that mean something. Both need a price per call.

#### Decision

Each model in `apps/litellm/config.yaml` gets a price in its `model_info`: what the nearest model Azure sells
would cost, in USD, from the Azure Retail Prices API (East US, Global Standard), read 2026-11-06.

| Local model | Azure model priced | Price |
|---|---|---|
| `qwen3.6-35b-a3b` | gpt-oss-120B (Azure sells no Qwen; the closest open-weight MoE it sells pay-per-token) | $0.15 in, $0.60 out per million tokens |
| `gemma-4-26b-a4b` | gpt-oss-120B (Azure sells no Gemma) | $0.15 in, $0.60 out per million tokens |
| `qwen3-embedding-0.6b` | text-embedding-3-small | $0.02 per million tokens |
| `flux.1-schnell` | FLUX1.1 [pro] (Azure has no schnell) | $0.04 an image |
| `ltx-2.5` | LTX-2.5 Pro image-to-video, 720p, through fal | $0.12 a second of clip |

LiteLLM's spend, key budgets and the UI's Costs column then read "what this would have cost on Azure". The
electricity estimate stays on the Grafana dashboard as what it really costs. Recheck the prices at least
once a year, and when a model changes.

#### Considered options

1. **Azure list prices for the nearest model** (chosen): the platform's cloud is Azure, the prices come from a
   public API anyone can rerun, and the comparison answers "what does the homelab save".
2. **The electricity cost per token** from the dashboard: the true marginal cost, but it moves with the
   tariff and the load, can't be set per call type, and is too small for a budget to mean anything.
3. **OpenAI's or a router's prices for the same open models** (for example a Qwen price on a hosting
   service): closer models, but not the platform's cloud, and the prices change often.
4. **Leave them at $0**: spend and budgets stay useless.

#### Consequences

- Good: per-key spend, budgets and the UI's Costs column work for the local models, and the figures compare
  with a real cloud bill.
- Good: the prices are in git with their source, so a check is one API query.
- Bad: the figures are notional. Nobody pays them, and a reader of LiteLLM's spend must know that.
- Bad: the matches are approximate. gpt-oss-120B is bigger than both chat models and FLUX1.1 [pro] is better
  than schnell, so the comparison flatters the homelab a little.
- Bad: LiteLLM 1.103 uses a deployment's own prices only when a token, second or query price is set, so the
  image model also carries a zero token price. Checked with mock calls on 1.103.1: chat, embedding, image and
  video calls each return the expected cost.

#### Related

- ADR 0022 (LiteLLM gateway), ADR 0029 (one key per caller, budgets)
- ADR 0081, 0056, 0054, 0066 (the models priced here)

## ADR-0090: Deployments keep at most 3 old ReplicaSets, set by a Kyverno MutatingPolicy that Argo CD ignores in its diffs
{: #adr-0090 }

<span class="adr-status adr-status-proposed">Proposed</span> <span class="adr-meta text-muted">2026-11-06 · `homelab-gitops`, `infra-local`</span>

#### Context

Every rollout leaves the previous ReplicaSet behind, scaled to 0, so `kubectl rollout undo` can return to
it. A Deployment keeps `spec.revisionHistoryLimit` of them, 10 when unset. On 2026-11-06 the clusters held
87 ReplicaSets at 0 replicas (wardens 28, gpu 21, platform 19, mon 9, ai 6, core 4). They run nothing and
cost only API objects, but they clutter Headlamp's resource map and `kubectl get rs`.

Our own manifests already set 3. The rest come from Helm charts: about 90 Deployments on platform, mon, ai,
gpu and wardens keep 10. Many of those charts (Kyverno, kube-state-metrics, KEDA, Temporal, external-dns,
the OTel operator, the model apps) write the 10 into the manifest themselves, so Argo CD owns the field.

Rollback here is a `git revert` in homelab-gitops, which Argo CD applies; a `kubectl rollout undo` would be
undone by self-heal within minutes. The old ReplicaSets are not part of how we roll back.

#### Decision

1. **A Kyverno `MutatingPolicy`, `limit-revision-history`**, in `apps/kyverno-policies` (so on every cluster
   labelled `homelab/addons=true`: platform, mon, ai, gpu, wardens), lowers a Deployment's missing or higher
   `revisionHistoryLimit` to 3 at admission. It never raises one, so k3s's add-ons keep 0. With
   `mutateExisting`, such a request also lowers every other Deployment above the limit; the Deployment
   controller then deletes the extra ReplicaSets. Kyverno's background controller gets get, list, watch,
   update and patch on Deployments for that.
2. **Argo CD ignores `.spec.revisionHistoryLimit` on every Deployment** in its diffs
   (`resource.customizations.ignoreDifferences.apps_Deployment` in `argocd-cm`, infra-local's Argo CD values).
   Without it, charts that write 10 would show OutOfSync, and self-heal would reapply 10 against Kyverno.
3. **Kyverno's own Deployments**, which Kyverno doesn't mutate, get 3 from the `kyverno` ApplicationSet's
   chart values. `kube-system` (Traefik, from k3s) stays out of scope.
4. The limit is 3, matching what our own manifests already set.
5. **Deployments an operator owns are left out** (added 2026-11-14): one with a controller `ownerReference`
   is neither a trigger nor a target. Its operator writes the spec back on every reconcile, so mutating it is
   a loop. The OpenTelemetry operator's `cluster-targetallocator`, the only such Deployment on the clusters,
   was rewritten 1-2 times a second from the policy's first sync: 1.25-1.6 million generations per cluster,
   about 1,000 pending UpdateRequests at a time (about 990,000 created and deleted on platform since k3s last
   started), k3s at 1.1-2.4 cores and 2.5-3.4 GiB on each Kyverno cluster against 5% and 1 GiB on core, and
   SQLite datastores of 0.7-1.5 GiB against 37 MiB. It keeps its operator's 10; it has one ReplicaSet.

#### Considered options

1. **Kyverno mutation plus an Argo CD ignore rule** (chosen): one place covers every chart, today's and
   future ones, and the existing ReplicaSets go without a manual step.
2. **Set the value in each chart's Helm values**: no new moving parts, but about 20 charts name the setting
   differently (some not at all), and every new chart has to remember it.
3. **A scheduled job that deletes ReplicaSets at 0 replicas**: works around the setting instead of using it,
   and needs delete rights on ReplicaSets in every namespace.
4. **Leave the default**: harmless, but the clutter is what prompted this.

#### Consequences

- Good: at most 3 old ReplicaSets per Deployment on the five clusters, with no per-chart work.
- Good: `kubectl rollout undo` still has 3 revisions for a break-glass rollback.
- Bad: Argo CD no longer shows a change to `revisionHistoryLimit` in git as OutOfSync; it still applies on
  the app's next sync.
- Bad: the policy changes objects that Helm charts render, so the live value differs from the chart's
  manifest. The README and this ADR say why.
- Bad: core (no Kyverno) keeps the chart defaults.
- Bad: a mutation that an operator undoes never settles, and nothing alerts on it: it showed up only as k3s
  CPU and memory (decision 5). Operator-owned Deployments keep their operator's value.

#### Related

- ADR 0033 (Kyverno admission policies), ADR 0038 (Argo CD)

## ADR-0091: The wardens agents' first MCP server is web search on wardens, behind mcp-guard, reached only through LiteLLM's MCP gateway
{: #adr-0091 }

<span class="adr-status adr-status-proposed">Proposed</span> <span class="adr-meta text-muted">2026-11-06 · `homelab-gitops`, `platform-wardens`, `infra-local`</span>

#### Context

platform-wardens decision 0031 says how agents make MCP calls:
- through a gateway, with an MCP-only credential;
- with a deny-by-default allow list per agent and tool, generated from the manifests;
- with the run's trace id on every call;
- with no token forwarded to a server.

On the homelab, the gateway is the existing LiteLLM (ADR 0022) with its `mcp_jwt_signer` guardrail. Nothing used it yet. To test tool calls, identity and tool blocking for real, the agents need a server.

Web search needs no credentials and has only read tools, and more than one agent can use it. The reviewed third-party server, `mcp-searxng` 2.7.0, can't verify LiteLLM's tokens: its OAuth mode wants claims LiteLLM doesn't send, and it has no rules per agent. So platform-wardens built a small guard, `mcp-guard`, to run beside it.

#### Decision

**`apps/mcp-web-search` on `wardens`:** one pod, three containers, namespace `mcp-web-search`.
- **`mcp-guard`** (platform-wardens image, Zot `platform/mcp-guard`, signed) is the only listener, on `:8080`. For each request it:
  - verifies LiteLLM's RS256 token against `https://llm.ai.home.cloudutsuk.com/.well-known/jwks.json` (`iss`, `aud: mcp`, expiry);
  - takes the agent from `act.sub`, the caller's LiteLLM team;
  - checks the agent and tool against `allowlist.yaml` and the token's `scope`;
  - requires `x-wardens-run` and `traceparent`, and filters `tools/list`;
  - forwards with mcp-searxng's own token.

  It writes one JSON log line per request and serves Prometheus metrics (a ServiceMonitor).
- **`mcp-searxng` 2.7.0** (docker.io, digest-pinned, reviewed in platform-wardens `framework/config/mcp-servers.yaml`) listens on `127.0.0.1:3000`, hardened, with a static token generated in the cluster and known only to the guard.
- **Its own SearXNG** on `127.0.0.1:8888`, with the same settings as `apps/searxng`. That way `wardens` doesn't depend on the `platform` cluster for search.
- **Ingress** `https://mcp-web-search.wardens.home.cloudutsuk.com` (tailnet only). The only caller that can pass the guard is LiteLLM.
- **NetworkPolicy:**
  - in: Traefik and the monitoring collector, to the guard only;
  - out: DNS, LiteLLM's address (172.30.0.4) on 443 for the JWKS, and the internet on 80/443 except the private, CGNAT, link-local and loopback ranges. A page read can't reach the homelab even if mcp-searxng's own URL check failed.
- `allowlist.yaml` is a copy of platform-wardens `deploy/mcp/web-search.allowlist.yaml`, which is generated from the agents' manifests.

**`apps/litellm`:**
- `mcp_servers.web_search` points at that URL, with `extra_headers: [x-wardens-run, traceparent]`.
- The `mcp_jwt_signer` guardrail runs on MCP calls only: issuer `https://llm.ai.home.cloudutsuk.com`, audience `mcp`, 60-second tokens.
- `MCP_JWT_SIGNING_KEY` comes from `kv/ai/litellm` `mcp_jwt_signing_key` (infra-local `host/litellm/configure.sh`).

**The agents** (stack-author, terraform-drift, incident-triage, change-correlator) get two settings:
- `WARDENS_MCP_URL` (LiteLLM's `/mcp`);
- `WARDENS_MCP_API_KEY`, their MCP-only key, from infra-local `host/wardens/mcp-keys.sh`. That script creates one team per agent and one key per agent, both granted only the agent's servers and tools, and with `no-default-models`.

LiteLLM blocks a tool an agent isn't granted first, and the guard blocks it again on its own.

#### Considered options

1. **A second LiteLLM, or agentgateway, as the MCP gateway:** rejected in platform-wardens decision 0031. The existing LiteLLM signs each call with the agent's team, and a guard at the server checks it.
2. **mcp-searxng alone, with its static token held by LiteLLM:** rejected. The server couldn't tell agents apart, and LiteLLM would be the only check.
3. **Use `platform`'s SearXNG (`apps/searxng`):** possible, but it would make `wardens` depend on another cluster and need a cross-cluster path. A second small SearXNG costs one container.
4. **No Ingress; reach the guard through a cluster-internal path from `ai`:** there's no cross-cluster service network. The tailnet Ingress plus the guard's token check is the same pattern the other wardens endpoints use.

#### Consequences

- **Good:** each tool call shows the agent and the run in three places, which share the run's trace id:
  - LiteLLM's key spend log;
  - the guard's log line and metrics;
  - the agent's run record.

  A tool an agent isn't granted is refused twice, and a server never sees an agent's key. The guard and the pattern can be reused for the next third-party server.
- **Bad:**
