  - The guard depends on LiteLLM's JWKS. If LiteLLM is down, no call can be made anyway.
  - All MCP servers behind LiteLLM share one audience (`mcp`). Short token lifetimes, unique tool names and the guard's `scope` check limit replay between servers.
  - The allow list is copied by hand from platform-wardens when manifests change.
  - Search engines may throttle the homelab's egress address on busy days.

#### Related

- ADR 0022 (LiteLLM), ADR 0055 (SearXNG), ADR 0069 to 0072 (wardens cluster, agents' charts and identities)
- platform-wardens decision 0031 and `docs/plans/mcp-web-search.md` (phase 4)

## ADR-0092: Policy Reporter shows Kyverno's reports, with a core on every cluster and one UI on platform
{: #adr-0092 }

<span class="adr-status adr-status-proposed">Proposed</span> <span class="adr-meta text-muted">2026-11-06 · `homelab-gitops`, `infra-local`</span>

#### Context

Kyverno (ADR 0033) writes a PolicyReport for every checked resource on platform, mon, ai, gpu and wardens.
Today the only ways to read them are `kubectl get policyreports` on each cluster, and the Kyverno dashboard's
counts in Grafana. Nothing shows, in one place, which resource fails which policy on which cluster, which is
what a policy change in Audit mode, or a new exception, needs.

[Policy Reporter](https://kyverno.github.io/policy-reporter/) (Kyverno project, Apache-2.0) has two parts in
one chart: a core per cluster that reads the reports and serves metrics and a REST API, and a UI that can read
several cores. Kyverno itself is installed by Argo CD, not by infra-local's Terragrunt (ADR 0038 keeps
infra-local to the bootstrap releases).

#### Decision

1. **An ApplicationSet, `policy-reporter`**, next to `kyverno`, installs the upstream chart (3.11.2, through
   Zot's ghcr.io mirror) in namespace `policy-reporter` on every cluster labelled `homelab/addons=true`, in
   the `security` project. Values: `apps/policy-reporter/values.yaml` for every cluster, and
   `values-<cluster>.yaml` on top where it exists (a second, git source).
2. **The core runs on every cluster** with the REST API and metrics on, both behind basic auth. Its API is
   published at `https://policy-reporter-api.<cluster>.home.cloudutsuk.com` (Traefik, Let's Encrypt), on the
   tailnet only. One credential for every cluster, in `kv/shared/policy-reporter`.
3. **One UI, on platform**, at `https://policy-reporter.platform.home.cloudutsuk.com`. It reads platform's
   core in the cluster and the other clusters through their API hostnames, each listed in `ui.clusters`.
4. **Sign-in through Keycloak**, with a confidential client `policy-reporter` and a gate for
   `platform-admins/editors/viewers` and `policy-reporter-admin/editor/viewer` (ADR 0078). The UI is
   read-only: the button that generates PolicyExceptions stays off, since exceptions are written in git
   (`apps/kyverno-policies/templates/exceptions.yaml`).
5. **Metrics without resource names** (custom labels: namespace, policy, rule, result, severity, source),
   scraped every 60 s. The chart's Grafana dashboards are off; dashboards come from `dashboards/` (ADR 0027).
6. Out of scope for now: the Kyverno plugin (policy details pages), notification targets (Slack, Loki) and a
   public name behind Cloudflare Access.

#### Considered options

1. **A Terragrunt `helm_release` per cluster in infra-local**: rejected. Kyverno, its policies and every other
   add-on are Argo CD's, and a reporter in a different tool from what it reports on would split one
   component's upgrades and drift across two places.
2. **A UI on every cluster**: five sign-in clients and five pages to visit; no API exposed between clusters.
   Rejected for the single page.
3. **Metrics only (no UI)**: the counts are already in Grafana; what's missing is the list of failing
   resources, which needs the UI.
4. **The UI reaching the cores through the Kubernetes API proxy** instead of Ingresses: would need a
   kubeconfig for every cluster in platform; an Ingress with basic auth is what the chart supports.

#### Consequences

- Good: one page for every cluster's policy results, signed in like every other app; per-policy pass and fail
  counts in Mimir.
- Good: a new addons cluster gets the core with no change; the UI needs one line in `ui.clusters`.
- Bad: each cluster now serves its policy results over HTTPS on the tailnet, guarded by one shared basic-auth
  credential. They name resources and policies, not secrets. Rotating means a new value in OpenBao and a
  restart of the cores and the UI.
- Bad: the core keeps its results in SQLite on an emptyDir; after a restart it re-reads the reports, so the
  UI's history starts again.

#### Related

- ADR 0027 (dashboards as code), 0033 (Kyverno policies), 0038 (Argo CD), 0078 (per-app access groups),
  0090 (revision history)

## ADR-0093: Phoenix runs internal-only in ai, fed with LLM spans by the OTLP gateway, LiteLLM and the wardens agents first
{: #adr-0093 }

<span class="adr-status adr-status-proposed">Proposed</span> <span class="adr-meta text-muted">2026-11-06 · `homelab-gitops`, `infra-local`, `platform-wardens`</span>

#### Context

ADR 0021 chose Arize Phoenix for LLM and RAG traces. It was written before three changes:

- ADR 0037 replaced the planned public UIs (`phoenix.cloudutsuk.com` behind Cloudflare Access) with private
  access over Tailscale; every UI signs in with Keycloak (ADR 0028).
- `rag-api` and `rag-ingest`, the first sources ADR 0021 named, aren't built yet (Phase 5 steps 3 and 4).
  The `sre-agent` became the `platform-wardens` agents, which already export OTel GenAI spans (semantic
  conventions 1.37) to the OTLP gateway in `mon`.
- Phoenix 21 converts `gen_ai.*` spans to its own OpenInference model when it ingests them, so the agents'
  spans show as LLM and tool spans without new instrumentation.

Some prompts carry personal data: Hermes reads the owner's mail and calendars, and job-hunter reads CVs and
job mail.

#### Decision

- **Phoenix 21.0.0** (non-root image, amd64 and arm64) in `ai`, namespace `phoenix`, one pod, at
  `https://phoenix.ai.home.cloudutsuk.com` on the tailnet only. No public hostname.
- **Data** in `ai-db`, database and owner role `phoenix` (ADR 0035). Phoenix's working folder is an `emptyDir`.
- **Sign-in** through Keycloak's `homelab` realm, client `phoenix`, auto-login, password sign-in off.
  The `platform-admins` and `phoenix-admin` groups map to ADMIN, every other user who passes the realm's
  gate to VIEWER.
- **Writes** come only from the OTLP gateway: a second trace pipeline, `traces/phoenix`, keeps spans that
  carry a `gen_ai.*` or `openinference.span.kind` attribute, names the Phoenix project after the span's
  `service.name` when the span doesn't name one, and sends them over OTLP HTTP with `PHOENIX_ADMIN_SECRET`
  as the bearer token. Tempo's pipeline doesn't change, so both stores share trace IDs. Services keep one
  exporter.
- **First sources:** the LiteLLM gateway (its `arize_phoenix` callback, pointed at the OTLP gateway, so
  every model call on the platform is traced) and the `platform-wardens` agents (unchanged). RAG traces
  follow with `rag-api` and `rag-ingest` (roadmap step 6b).
- **Content:** prompts and answers are kept, except for the Hermes and job-hunter virtual keys, whose
  message content LiteLLM masks; their spans still carry model, tokens, latency and cost (owner decision,
  2026-11-06).
- **Retention:** 30 days for every project (`PHOENIX_DEFAULT_RETENTION_POLICY_DAYS`, owner decision,
  2026-11-06).
- **No outbound calls of its own:** `PHOENIX_ALLOW_EXTERNAL_RESOURCES=false` (no web fonts, telemetry or
  price downloads), the built-in agent assistant off, and no LLM providers configured in the playground.

This amends ADR 0021's hostname, access and first sources; its choice of Phoenix over Langfuse stands.

#### Considered options

1. **Each service exports to Phoenix and to the gateway**: two exporters per service and Phoenix's API key in
   every namespace. Rejected.
2. **The gateway sends every span to Phoenix**: HTTP, database and Kubernetes spans would fill `ai-db`
   with traces Phoenix can't show any better than Tempo. Rejected.
3. **A filtered second pipeline on the gateway** (chosen).
4. **A Phoenix system API key for the gateway**: it must be created in the UI after the first start, so
   it can't be seeded with the other secrets. The admin secret is a static value OpenBao can generate.
5. **Public hostname behind Cloudflare Access**: not needed for one user on the tailnet (ADR 0037).

#### Consequences

- Good: every model call on the platform is visible with its prompt, tokens and latency from the first
  sync, before the RAG services exist.
- Good: one place decides what reaches Phoenix (the gateway's filter), and one secret lets it write.
- Bad: a dropped parent span (an HTTP span above an LLM span) leaves the LLM span as a root in Phoenix;
  the full tree stays in Tempo.
- Bad: the admin secret is all-powerful in Phoenix; it lives only in OpenBao and the gateway's Secret.

#### Related

- [ADR 0021](#adr-0021)
- [ADR 0028](#adr-0028)
- [ADR 0035](#adr-0035)
- [ADR 0037](#adr-0037)
- Plan

## ADR-0094: Hermes posts the daily briefing to Flashy as cards stored in Cloudflare D1, with Web Push reminders that come more often until it's read
{: #adr-0094 }

<span class="adr-status adr-status-proposed">Proposed</span> <span class="adr-meta text-muted">2026-11-08 · `homelab-gitops`, `infra-local`, `flashy`</span>

#### Context

Hermes (ADR 0064) builds a morning brief from Gmail, Google Calendar, the Outlook calendar and its own reminders,
and sends it to the owner's WhatsApp self-chat. The owner wants the brief to also appear in Flashy, the personal
flashcards PWA (`cloudutsukpilot/flashy`, `https://flashy.cloudutsuk.com`):

- one card per part of the brief: Gmail, Outlook meetings, pending work, and others;
- the cards must be marked read before the other flashcards can be browsed;
- while they're unread, the phone is reminded again and again, at shorter and shorter intervals.

Flashy is a static site on Cloudflare Pages behind Cloudflare Access (admins only), with no server and no
account: progress lives on the device. Hermes runs in `platform` and reaches the internet, but nothing in the
homelab is reachable from the internet except through the Cloudflare tunnel (ADR 0058).

The brief carries personal data: subjects and senders of mail, meeting titles, to-dos.

#### Decision

1. **Storage and API on Cloudflare.** Flashy gets Pages Functions and a D1 database (`flashy`). Hermes PUTs the
   day's brief as JSON (`{date, cards: [{id, kind, title, body, items}]}`) to
   `https://flashy.cloudutsuk.com/api/ingest/briefing/<date>`. Flashy reads the latest brief from
   `/api/briefing/latest` and marks cards read with `POST /api/briefing/<date>/cards/<id>/read`, all same-origin
   behind the existing Access app. Briefs older than 30 days are deleted on ingest.
2. **Hermes authenticates as a machine.** A path-scoped Access application on `flashy.cloudutsuk.com/api/ingest`
   admits only an Access **service token** (`hermes-flashy`), and the Function also requires a bearer ingest
   token. `access.sh` learns `allow: service` and keeps the service token in OpenBao.
3. **Reminders by Web Push.** The phone subscribes (Settings, once) with Flashy's VAPID public key; the
   subscription is stored in D1. Pushes carry no payload: the service worker fetches the brief itself (with the
   Access cookie) to write the notification, and falls back to a generic "Briefing unread" if the session has
   expired, so no brief text passes through the browser vendor's push service. Unread reminders go out when
   the brief arrives, then after 2 h, 1 h, 30 min and 15 min, then every 15 min, never between 22:00 and 07:00
   Asia/Kolkata, and stop once every card is read.
4. **The reminder clock lives in the homelab.** Pages Functions have no schedule, so a CronJob in `platform`
   (`apps/hermes`, every 5 minutes) calls `/api/ingest/tick` with the same service token. The Function decides
   whether a reminder is due, so the CronJob only knocks.
5. **Hermes composes, a script posts.** A script in git (`apps/hermes/flashy-briefing/post_briefing.py`, copied
   into `/opt/data/scripts` by `setup` like the Outlook scripts) validates the JSON and PUTs it. A skill tells
   Hermes how to build the cards: the morning-brief cron job calls the script after sending to WhatsApp.

#### Considered options

1. **Pages Function + D1 (chosen).** Same-origin with Flashy, so one Access app and no CORS; works when the
   homelab is down (the last brief stays readable, it just doesn't update); least new code.
2. **Serve Flashy from `platform` with a small API, through the tunnel.** The brief would never leave the
   homelab, but Flashy would go down with the homelab, and it would need an image, a chart and Kyverno-compliant
   manifests for what is today a static site.
3. **A brief API in `platform` on its own hostname, Flashy stays on Pages.** Data stays home, but Flashy would
   call a second Access application cross-site: Access cookies are per hostname, so sign-in and CORS get fiddly.
4. **Reminders over WhatsApp from Hermes, or in-app only.** WhatsApp needs no new plumbing but duplicates the
   brief's channel; in-app only can't remind when Flashy is closed. The owner chose real phone notifications.
5. **A Cloudflare Worker with a cron trigger for the reminder clock.** Runs without the homelab, but needs a
   Workers deploy and a token with Workers Scripts Write; the homelab is already required to produce the brief.

#### Consequences

- Good: the brief is on the phone as cards, gated before study, and the phone nags until it's read.
- Good: Flashy keeps working offline for flashcards; only the brief needs the network.
- Bad: brief text is stored in Cloudflare D1 (the owner's account, behind Access), not only in the homelab.
  Kept for 30 days.
- Bad: no reminders while the homelab is down (no tick). The last brief still shows.
- Bad: new secrets to keep: the Access service token, the ingest token and the VAPID private key.

#### Related

- ADR 0058 (public access through Cloudflare Access), ADR 0064 (Hermes as a personal assistant)
- Plan: `flashy/docs/briefing-plan.md`

#### Amendments

- 2026-11-08, school mail: a second job (School mail, 06:45; an 18:00 run was dropped the same day, once a day is enough) posts a `school` card to the same day.
  Posts now **merge** by default (the posted cards replace those with the same ids, the day's other cards stay),
  so the Morning brief and the school job don't wipe each other. The owner asked for school events and holidays to
  go on Google Calendar **without asking first**, the one exception to "changing Google Calendar asks first"
  (ADR 0064); they're marked with a private property so later runs don't add them twice.
- 2026-11-08, tech news: a third job (Tech news, 07:15 and 18:15) posts a `news` card (kind `news`): the top 5
  trending stories across AI, agentic AI, DevOps, platform engineering and SRE/observability. Sources are a feed
  list kept in git plus the Hacker News front page and SearXNG for trending signals; the script, not the model,
  fetches them, and remembers what was posted so the evening card has only new stories. No links on the card
  (source names only), like the other cards.
- 2026-11-08, plan last: the owner wants the plan for the day at the bottom, built from every other card. The
  Morning brief no longer writes it; a Plan for the day job at 07:30 reads the day's cards back (a new
  `GET /api/ingest/briefing/:date`, same credentials as the PUT) and posts the `plan` card, which Flashy always
  stores last.

## ADR-0095: Private repos' CI runs only on the homelab runners, with a third scale set for the cloud IaC repos
{: #adr-0095 }

<span class="adr-status adr-status-proposed">Proposed</span> <span class="adr-meta text-muted">2026-11-09 · `homelab-gitops`, `terragrunt-shared-module`, `infra-azure`, `infra-aws`, `infra-gcp`, `infra-github`, `infra-cloudflare`, `infra-local`, `cicd-triage-lab`, `flashy`, `job-hunter-agent`, `shorts-studio`, `reusable-workflows`</span>

#### Context

The org is on GitHub's Free plan: 2,000 GitHub-hosted minutes a month for private repos, and a $0 spending
limit. On 2026-11-09 the private repos had used 2,019 minutes, and from 15:29 UTC GitHub stopped starting
`ubuntu-latest` jobs ("your spending limit needs to be increased"). The six `infra-*` drift-scan PRs, every
scheduled drift scan and the drift agent's trigger job stopped with them. Public repos (the blog) don't count
against the allowance.

Most of the minutes were short jobs, because GitHub bills each job as at least one minute. Per workflow,
1 to 9 November:

| Repo | Workflow | Minutes | Jobs |
|---|---|---|---|
| infra-azure | terragrunt-cicd | 428 | 292 |
| infra-github | terragrunt-cicd | 289 | 260 |
| terragrunt-shared-module | compatibility test | 211 | 208 |
| cicd-triage-lab | five language pipelines | 355 | 336 |
| homelab-gitops | adr-check, dashboards-check | 201 | 201 |
| infra-local | terragrunt-cicd helper jobs | 100 | 100 |
| terragrunt-shared-module | code-quality, python-quality, terragrunt-cicd, docs-pages | 103 | 86 |
| flashy | ci, deploy | 100 | 100 |
| infra-aws, -gcp, -cloudflare | terragrunt-cicd | 140 | 140 |
| job-hunter-agent, reusable-workflows, shorts-studio | ci, actionlint | 64 | 53 |

Only `homelab-services`, `self-service-portal`, `platform-wardens` and `build-image.yaml` ran on the
`homelab` scale set. The cloud `infra-*` repos ran their Terragrunt jobs on `ubuntu-latest`
(`iac_runner` in their `config.yaml`), and the Terragrunt workflow template hard-codes `ubuntu-latest` for its
helper jobs (Set Build Variables, Detect Changes, Manual Dispatch, the drift scan and notifications), even in
`infra-local`, whose plans run on `homelab-iac`.

Two gaps stood in the way:

- **Tools.** Neither runner image has `yq` or `gh`. The helper jobs read `config.yaml` with `yq` and look PRs up
  with `gh`. The shared module's `resolve-credentials` reads `common_vars.yaml` with `yq` and hides errors with
  `2>/dev/null || true`, so on a runner without `yq` it picks no cloud credentials and doesn't fail.
  `dashboards-check` needs `kubectl kustomize` and runs `promtool` in Docker. The IaC image also has no Azure
  CLI, and the shared module runs `az login` / `az account show` for every `--cloud azure` or `entra` run
  (found 2026-11-10: every infra-azure plan on `homelab-cloud-iac` failed with "Required tool not found on
  PATH: az"). Cloudflare, AWS and GCP runs don't call it; their Azure state backend authenticates without it.
- **Where the cloud IaC jobs run.** `homelab-iac` is registered with `infra-local` alone and carries root on
  the host (Docker over mTLS) and the cluster kubeconfigs (ADR 0040). The Free plan has only the Default runner
  group, so a scale set registered with the org is open to every private repo.

#### Decision

- **Every private repo's CI runs on the homelab runners.** GitHub-hosted runners stay only for public repos
  (the blog), which are free, and which ADR 0019 keeps off self-hosted runners. A job stays on `ubuntu-latest`
  only where it needs something the runners can't give it, and is listed below.
- **Three scale sets** in `platform`:
  - `homelab` (org): build, test and lint jobs, and the Terragrunt workflow's helper jobs.
  - `homelab-iac` (`infra-local` only): unchanged (ADR 0040, 0059, 0062).
  - `homelab-cloud-iac` (org, new): the Terragrunt plans and applies of `infra-azure`, `-aws`, `-gcp`,
    `-github` and `-cloudflare`, and the shared module's compatibility test. Same image as `homelab-iac`
    (the IaC tools at `/opt/iac`) and the same memory gate, but no host access: no Docker, no kubeconfig, no
    state-backend credentials in the pod. Each job brings its cloud credentials from its own repository's
    secrets, as it did on `ubuntu-latest`. It has its own Terragrunt provider cache, because any private repo
    can write to it. 0 to 3 runners.
- **Tools.** `yq` and `gh` go into both tool sets (`ci/tools`, `ci/iac-tools`); `kubectl` and `promtool` into
  `ci/tools`. `scripts/dashboards.py` uses a `promtool` on the PATH before Docker. The Azure CLI goes into
  `ci/iac-tools`, hash-locked (`requirements-az.txt`) in its own venv at `/opt/iac/az` with `/opt/iac/bin/az`,
  so its ~150 packages can't clash with the shared module's; it adds about 690 MB to the IaC image.
- **How the workflows pick a runner.**
  - Plain workflows: `runs-on: homelab`.
  - The Terragrunt template and the shared module's reusable workflows: helper jobs use
    `${{ vars.CI_RUNNER || 'ubuntu-latest' }}`, and a **repository** variable `CI_RUNNER=homelab` on the shared
    module and each `infra-*` repo sets it (private repos on GitHub Free can't read organization variables).
    The `ubuntu-latest` fallback keeps forks without self-hosted runners working, as `IAC_RUNNER` already does.
  - The cloud `infra-*` repos set `iac_runner: homelab-cloud-iac` in `config.yaml`.
- **Docker builds without a Docker daemon.** Jobs that only check that an image builds (`cicd-triage-lab`'s
  image jobs, `job-hunter-agent`'s `docker` job) use `docker buildx` with the remote driver against the
  BuildKit in `platform`, as `build-image.yaml` does, and keep the result in the build cache.
- **`docker://` actions** become the tool's release binary, checked against its published sha256
  (`reusable-workflows`' `actionlint`).
- **Integration tests that need a database run it inside the job, not in Docker.** `job-hunter-agent`'s
  `supabase` job started with `supabase start` (needs a Docker daemon) and stayed on GitHub-hosted runners
  until GitHub refused hosted jobs over billing (2026-11-13), which blocked its image. Since 2026-11-14
  (job-hunter-agent#37) it runs on `homelab`: `scripts/local_supabase.py` starts Postgres 17 and PostgREST
  from their release binaries (pinned by sha256) with Supabase's API roles, about 30 s a run.
- **Stay on GitHub-hosted:** the Copilot coding agent and Dependabot.

#### Considered options

1. **Raise the spending limit and keep GitHub-hosted runners.** Costs money every month for jobs the homelab
   can run, and a forgotten limit stops CI again.
2. **Run the cloud IaC jobs on `homelab`.** The generic image has no Terraform or Terragrunt, so every
   ephemeral job downloads them (and the provider plugins) again; the shared module's `setup-environment`
   would do it, slowly.
3. **Register `homelab-iac` with the org.** Hands Docker on the host and the cluster kubeconfigs to every
   private repo's workflows, which ADR 0040 exists to prevent.
4. **A third scale set with the IaC image and no host access** (chosen).

#### Consequences

- Good: CI no longer depends on GitHub's allowance; the blocked drift scans and PRs run again.
- Good: each Terragrunt job gets the baked IaC tools and a warm provider cache instead of downloads.
- Good: `yq` on every runner makes credential resolution work everywhere, instead of failing quietly.
- Bad: CI capacity is the DGX's (later the homelab PC's) spare memory. A burst of `infra-*` runs waits for
  the memory gate and the 3-runner cap; ARCScaleSetAtCapacity and the dashboard show it.
- Bad: cloud credentials (Azure prod among them) are now used on a runner any private repo can pick. They
  still come from each repo's own secrets, so a job in another repo can't read them, but the pods share the
  node and the BuildKit.
- Bad: two images to rebuild when a tool changes, as before.

#### Related

- ADR 0019 (self-hosted runners for private repos only), ADR 0040 (repo-scoped `homelab-iac`)
- ADR 0044 and 0059 (runner images and the memory gate), ADR 0062 (provider cache), ADR 0086 (runner alerts)
- Plan and progress: docs/plans/ci-off-github-hosted-runners.md

## ADR-0096: Shared-module READMEs carry data blocks for agents, and each organisation states its preferences in a profile
{: #adr-0096 }

<span class="adr-status adr-status-proposed">Proposed</span> <span class="adr-meta text-muted">2026-11-10 · `terragrunt-shared-module`, `platform-wardens`, `infra-azure`</span>

#### Context

The Stack Author agent writes a stack's `resources.yaml` from a module's README (the first 9,000 characters),
`example_resources.yaml` and the keys `terragrunt.hcl` reads. The terraform-drift agent reads `yaml import-spec` blocks
from Azure and Entra READMEs. Apart from import specs, READMEs are free-form: 20-plus heading variants, parameter
tables that drift from what the unit reads (the storage account README documented a key the unit never reads and the
wrong private endpoint default), and nothing about expected drift.

Organisations using the shared module can have different rules: one may require private endpoints, while the
cloudutsuk tenant prefers service endpoints to avoid private endpoint cost. Today nothing records those rules, so
an agent or a person picks per stack.

#### Decision

- Every module README follows one format (shared module `docs/module-readme-format.md`): fixed `##` sections in a fixed
  order, prose and tables for people, and fenced data blocks for tools: `yaml module` (key, types, shape,
  dependencies), `yaml module-preferences` (which org preferences the module supports and the `resources.yaml` keys
  each value sets), `yaml import-spec` (unchanged), `yaml drift-notes` (expected drift and the action). A marker line
  opts a README in; `tools/check_module_readme.py` and the module validator check it against the unit and `main.tf`.
- A **preference catalogue** (`config/preferences/catalogue.yaml`) is the general list of choices (public access,
  private endpoint vs service endpoint, NSG default, local auth, encryption key, TLS, diagnostics, zone redundancy,
  deletion protection). An **org profile** (`config/preferences/orgs/<org>.yaml`) sets a value per preference and per
  resource type; a consumer repo picks it with `org_profile` in `config.yaml`. Module READMEs never name an org.
- Precedence: a value written in the stack, then the profile's per-resource value, then its org value, then the module
  default. Stack Author writes the keys explicitly, so plans don't depend on the profile. Enforcement (OPA) comes later
  and reads the same files.

#### Considered options

1. **Org profile file, modules list the knobs** (chosen): one file per org, no README edit when an org or a rule
   changes; scales to 200 modules.
2. **A per-org table in every README**: readable in one place, but every README changes when an org is added, and org
   names spread through the module docs.
3. **Preferences only in each consumer repo**: no shared list of values, so modules can't say how to meet them.
4. **A separate `module.yaml` per module instead of README blocks**: cleaner for tools, but two files to keep in step
   and the README stays free-form for people.

#### Consequences

- Good: agents parse exact data instead of prose; a checker catches README keys the unit never reads; org rules are
  stated once and reviewed like code.
- Good: drift notes let the drift agent tell expected differences from real drift.
- Bad: READMEs convert one by one; until then agents fall back to prose. Tracked in the shared module's
  `docs/plans/module-readme-format.md`.
- Bad: a preference can only be met if the module supports it (Storage has no customer-managed key support); the
  agent warns rather than blocks.

#### Related

- Shared module `docs/importing_resources.md` (import specs, the first data block)

## ADR-0097: Zot's UI signs people in through Keycloak; machines keep their htpasswd accounts
{: #adr-0097 }

<span class="adr-status adr-status-proposed">Proposed</span> <span class="adr-meta text-muted">2026-11-10 · `infra-local`, `homelab-gitops`</span>

#### Context

ADR 0034 said people would log in to Zot's UI through Keycloak, but that part was never configured. The login
page at `https://registry.core.home.cloudutsuk.com/login` offers only a username and password. Checked on
2026-11-10: the live `zot-config` ConfigMap has `http.auth.htpasswd` and no `http.auth.openid`, and
`/v2/_zot/ext/mgmt` (the endpoint the UI reads to decide which sign-in buttons to draw) reports
`{"auth": {"htpasswd": {}, "allowAnonymousAccess": true}}`. The `ui` and `search` extensions and
`http.externalUrl` were already set.

The only accounts are `admin`, `puller` and `ci-pusher`, shared passwords in `kv/core/zot`. Using the UI as a
person means using one of them. Machines depend on them and must keep working unchanged:

- CI pushes with crane and cosign as `ci-pusher` (ADR 0043).
- Kyverno adds the `zot-pull` secret (`puller`) to pods, and the nodes pull `mirror/*` anonymously through
  `registries.yaml`.

Access is assigned only with Keycloak groups (ADR 0078).

#### Decision

- **Zot gets an OpenID provider `oidc`** named Keycloak, issuer `https://auth.cloudutsuk.com/realms/homelab`,
  scopes `openid profile email`, `claimMapping` username `email`, groups `groups`. The redirect URI comes
  from `http.externalUrl`: `https://registry.core.home.cloudutsuk.com/zot/auth/callback/oidc`.
- **A confidential Keycloak client `zot`**, created by `host/keycloak/configure-realm.sh` like the others: its
  secret is generated into `kv/core/keycloak zot_client_secret` and set on the client. core's External Secrets
  can read `kv/core/*`, so the `zot-oidc` ExternalSecret reads it there with no copy. It renders Zot's
  `credentialsFile` (`credentials.json`) and `sessionKeysFile` (`session-keys.json`), mounted at
  `/secret-oidc`.
- **Groups to access.** `platform-admins` and `zot-admin` join Zot's `adminPolicy` (read, create, update,
  delete everywhere). `platform-editors`, `platform-viewers`, `zot-editor` and `zot-viewer` get read on every
  repository. Editors don't get push: images reach Zot only from CI, signed and pinned by digest (ADR 0033).
  The `zot` client has a Keycloak gate for those six groups, so nobody else can sign in; without the gate
  they would only get what anonymous gets (read on `mirror/*` and `charts/*`).
- **Machines are unchanged.** htpasswd stays on, with the same three accounts and policies, and the anonymous
  reads on `mirror/*` and `charts/*` stay.
- **A fourth machine account, `metrics` (added 2026-11-11).** It may read `/metrics` (`accessControl.metrics`)
  and no repository. mon's OTLP gateway scrapes Zot with it through the ingress, since `core` sends no telemetry
  of its own (ADR 0038), for the GC alerts in `alerts/platform/zot.yaml`. Its password is generated into
  `kv/core/zot metrics_password` and copied to `kv/mon/otlp-gateway zot_metrics` by infra-local
  `host/zot/configure-metrics.sh`. Anonymous metrics were the alternative; they would show every repository's
  name and size to anyone on the tailnet.
- **Sessions.** `secureSession: true` (Traefik terminates TLS, so Zot wouldn't set the Secure flag on its own).
  The session hash key is derived in the ExternalSecret template from the client secret
  (`sha256("zot-session:" + secret)`), so sessions survive a Zot restart without another secret to keep, and
  a new client secret signs everyone out.

#### Considered options

1. **OpenID provider in Zot (chosen)**: Zot has it built in (v2.1.21), maps the `groups` claim to its own
   policies, and the UI draws the button from the config.
2. **oauth2-proxy in front of the UI**: the UI and the registry API share one host and paths, so the proxy
   would have to let `/v2/` through untouched for machines, and Zot would still see every person as
   anonymous, with no admin role.
3. **Per-person htpasswd entries**: passwords outside Keycloak, assigned by hand, against ADR 0078.

#### Consequences

- Good: people sign in with Entra through Keycloak, and their role in Zot comes from the same groups as in
  every other app.
- Good: no change for CI, Kyverno's `zot-pull` or the nodes.
- Bad: Zot reads the issuer's discovery document when it starts and exits if it can't. A Zot restart now
  needs Keycloak up (Keycloak's image comes from quay.io directly when Zot is down, since containerd falls
  back to the upstream registry). After a cold start of `core`, Zot restarts until Keycloak is ready.
- Bad: if `kv/core/keycloak zot_client_secret` is missing, the `zot-oidc` secret isn't created and the Zot pod
  can't start. `configure-realm.sh` must run before this is applied.

#### Related

- [ADR 0034](#adr-0034): the registry; this builds its "people log in through Keycloak".
- [ADR 0078](#adr-0078): groups as the only place access is assigned.
- [ADR 0028](#adr-0028), [ADR 0058](#adr-0058): the issuer.

## ADR-0098: The shared-module framework is named PaC - Platform as Code, and its package is renamed with no compatibility alias
{: #adr-0098 }

<span class="adr-status adr-status-proposed">Proposed</span> <span class="adr-meta text-muted">2026-11-10 · `terragrunt-shared-module`, `infra-aws`, `infra-azure`, `infra-cloudflare`, `infra-gcp`, `infra-github`, `infra-local`, `platform-wardens`</span>

#### Context

The shared module's Python package, project and command carried another organisation's name, and its docs, defaults,
examples and tests named that organisation's GitHub orgs, domains, registries and secrets. The framework now
deploys to many providers and platforms (Azure, AWS, GCP, Cloudflare, GitHub, JFrog, Kubernetes, Helm, k3d), so "IaC"
undersells it, and the other organisation's names don't belong in it.

Every consumer repo's `entrypoint.py` imports the package by name. CI is unaffected (the reusable workflows copy the
shared module's own `entrypoint.py` over the consumer's), but a local run uses the consumer's copy with the sibling
shared module checkout. The shared module refreshes that copy only after it has imported the package, so after a
rename a consumer with the old file can't run locally until it gets the new one.

#### Decision

- The framework is **PaC - Platform as Code**. The package is `platform_as_code` (project `platform-as-code`, command
  `pac`, base error `PacError`).
- Every other organisation's name becomes a Cloudutsuk one: GitHub org `cloudutsukpilot`, `home.cloudutsuk.com` hosts,
  `CLOUDUTSUK_*` secrets and variables, `cloudutsuk-*` Kubernetes secrets. Only the `cloudutsuk` org profile remains.
- No compatibility alias: it would have to carry the old name. The shared module PR and the six consumer
  `entrypoint.py` PRs merge the same day instead, and local checkouts pull `main` before the next run. The entrypoint also drops its fallback to the pre-`src/` layout.
- The repository keeps its name (`terragrunt-shared-module`) for now; renaming it is a later decision.

#### Considered options

1. **Rename, merge the same day** (chosen): no old name left anywhere; only a local run with an out-of-date checkout
   fails.
2. **Rename with an alias package under the old name, removed later**: no failure window, but the old name stays in the
   repo for weeks and needs a third round of PRs.
3. **Consumers' entrypoint tries both names first**: same as 2, with the old name in six repos instead of one.
4. **Keep the name**: no work, but the name stays wrong for a multi-platform framework.

#### Consequences

- Good: one name for the framework everywhere, and no other organisation's names in it.
- Good: the terraform-drift, Stack Author and change-correlator agents need no code change: they read the shared
  module's `cloud/` tree, plan artifacts and `config.yaml` keys, none of which change.
- Bad: a local run fails until both the consumer and the sibling shared module checkout are on the new `main`.
- Bad: infra-azure changes the `github-repository` tag in place on its resources (to `cloudutsukpilot/infra-azure`).

#### Related

- ADR 0096
- Shared module plan: `docs/plans/rename-to-platform-as-code.md`

## ADR-0099: Dependency-Track keeps the inventory of every SBOM CI builds, on platform, with its database in platform-db
{: #adr-0099 }

<span class="adr-status adr-status-accepted">Accepted</span> <span class="adr-meta text-muted">2026-11-12 · `homelab-gitops`, `infra-local`, `homelab-services`</span>

#### Context

Every `main` build of `build-image.yaml` writes a CycloneDX SBOM per platform with Syft and attests it on the
image index in Zot (ADR 0033). Kyverno checks that the attestation exists, but nothing reads what is inside it:
there is no list of the components and licences that run on the platform, and a CVE published after a build is
only found if the image is rebuilt and Trivy runs again (ADR 0032). Roadmap Phase 3a step 5b asks for one
dashboard with every `main` image's components, licences and open CVEs, where a new CVE shows up without a
rebuild. The plan is `docs/plans/dependency-track.md`.

OWASP Dependency-Track (Apache-2.0) does that: it takes SBOMs over its API, keeps the inventory, and re-checks
it against advisory feeds on a schedule. Checked on 2026-11-12 for the release we would run:

- **Release 5.2.1** (2026-10-25), Helm chart `dependency-track` 2.9.0, published as OCI on ghcr.io and pulled
  through Zot's mirror (`mirror/ghcr.io/dependencytrack/helm-charts`). Version 5 needs only PostgreSQL: its
  work queue runs in the database, and the chart renders an api-server, a frontend and a PVC, with no broker.
- **Images** `dependencytrack/apiserver` and `dependencytrack/frontend` 5.2.1 are OCI indexes with linux/amd64
  and linux/arm64, and both resolve through Zot's `mirror/docker.io`. Both run as numeric non-root users
  (1000 and 101).
- **Secrets.** The api-server needs a database login and a key encryption key (KEK, 32 random bytes) for the
  secrets it stores in Postgres, such as feed tokens and notification webhooks. The chart doesn't generate
  either; it reads existing Secrets.
- **Admission.** The api-server's defaults already meet the restricted profile and a read-only root (it writes
  `/data`, a PVC, and `/tmp`). The frontend's default is a writable root, because its start script rewrites
  `static/config.json` (API and OIDC settings) and nginx renders `conf.d/default.conf` from a template. With the
  config file mounted read-only from a ConfigMap and an `emptyDir` on `/etc/nginx/conf.d`, it starts and serves
  the mounted config (tested with `docker run --read-only`, 2026-11-12); its script skips the rewrite when the
  file can't be written. A render of the draft values passes all six platform policies with the Kyverno CLI
  v1.19.1 (12 pass, 0 fail).
- **Sign-in.** The SPA runs the OIDC code flow itself (`OIDC_FLOW=code`, callback
  `/static/oidc-callback.html`); the api-server only validates the ID token against the issuer and client ID,
  provisions the user, and syncs teams from a claim.
- **Egress.** Version 5 refuses outbound connections outside `dt.outbound.allowed-destinations` (default
  `external,private`); the database and the OIDC issuer are always allowed.

#### Decision

- **Run Dependency-Track 5.2.1 on `platform`**, namespace `dependency-track`, from the upstream chart 2.9.0
  with values inline in the Application (ADR 0072 rule 3). Images by index digest. One api-server replica
  (`monolith` topology) with a 10 GiB `local-path` PVC; requests 250m CPU and 2 GiB, limit 3 GiB to start,
  resized from what mon shows after a week. Ingress
  `dependency-track.platform.home.cloudutsuk.com` (Traefik, cert-manager `letsencrypt`); the chart routes
  `/api` to the api-server and `/` to the frontend on one host, so the UI calls the API with relative URLs.
- **The database is `dtrack` in `platform-db`**, with its own owner role `dtrack` (ADR 0035), created by an
  init script for new data folders and once by hand on the existing one. platform-db already has host storage,
  nightly dumps and Velero coverage; a second Postgres would need all three again.
- **Secrets in OpenBao `kv/platform/dependency-track`**: `db_password`, `kek` (base64, 32 bytes, `config`
  mode), and `admin_password`, seeded with `host/openbao/seed-random.sh`. The `dtrack` password also goes to
  `kv/platform/platform-db dtrack_password` for the init script. ExternalSecrets render the chart's
  `dependency-track-db` and `dependency-track-kek` Secrets. The KEK can't be rotated in place in `config`
  mode; moving to a Tink keyset is a later change if rotation is needed. Losing the KEK loses only the secrets
  stored in Dependency-Track (feed tokens, webhooks), which are re-entered.
- **Feeds: NVD, GitHub Advisories, and OSV** for PyPI, npm, Go, Maven, Alpine, Debian and Ubuntu, set by
  infra-local `host/dependency-track/configure-feeds.sh`. Only GitHub Advisories needs a credential: a GitHub
  token with no permissions, kept in OpenBao `kv/platform/dependency-track github_token` and copied into
  Dependency-Track's own secret store (encrypted with the KEK). NVD needs no key in 5.x: it mirrors NVD's public
  JSON 2.0 feed files and has no key setting (checked on the live 5.2.1, 2026-11-12; corrected from "an NVD API
  key" in the first draft). Outbound destinations are narrowed to `external`.
- **OS packages are matched through their distribution's data, not NVD.** The SBOM copy uploaded to
  Dependency-Track drops the CPE of every `pkg:deb`, `pkg:apk` and `pkg:rpm` component (the attested SBOM in
  Zot keeps it). With the CPE, NVD's upstream version ranges flagged Debian packages whose fixes Debian had
  backported: on 2026-11-13 every critical in the Python images (OpenSSL and Perl, 25 criticals and 123 highs
  across 15 images) was such a false positive, all fixed in the versions we ship per Debian's tracker. Without
  it, OSV's Debian, Ubuntu and Alpine data (matched by package URL, backport-aware) is the only source for them,
  the same data Trivy's gate reads. Language packages keep NVD, GitHub and OSV.
- **Sign-in through Keycloak** with a **public client `dependency-track`** (PKCE, `groups` claim with bare
  group names), created by `host/keycloak/configure-realm.sh` and gated by its app groups (ADR 0078). The
  api-server has `DT_OIDC_*` set: issuer `https://auth.cloudutsuk.com/realms/homelab`, username claim
  `preferred_username`, user provisioning and team synchronisation on, teams claim `groups`. The frontend's
  `config.json` comes from a ConfigMap. Teams and their group mappings:

  | Team | Keycloak groups | Permissions |
  |---|---|---|
  | Administrators | `platform-admins`, `dependency-track-admin` | everything |
  | Triage | `platform-editors`, `dependency-track-editor` | view portfolio, view and audit vulnerabilities, view policy violations |
  | Viewers | `platform-viewers`, `dependency-track-viewer` | view portfolio, view vulnerabilities |
  | `ci-upload` | none (API key only) | `BOM_UPLOAD`, `PROJECT_CREATION_UPLOAD`, `PORTFOLIO_MANAGEMENT_CREATE`, `VIEW_PORTFOLIO` |

  The built-in `admin` account gets the random `admin_password` and is used only to create the teams and
  mappings and for break-glass.
- **Project model.** One parent project per image (`platform/<image>`), a **collection project**
  (`AGGREGATE_LATEST_VERSION_CHILDREN`) that CI creates on the image's first upload, and one child per platform
  (`platform/<image>/linux-amd64`, `.../linux-arm64`), version = the git SHA, uploaded with `isLatest=true` so the
  parent and the portfolio count only the newest version. A BOM upload can create a project only under a parent
  that already exists, so the `ci-upload` team also has `PORTFOLIO_MANAGEMENT_CREATE` (checked on 5.2.1,
  2026-11-12). A weekly job keeps the last five versions of each project active.
- **CI uploads on `main` only**, after the SBOMs are attested, to the in-cluster service with the `ci-upload`
  API key from `kv/ci/dependency-track`. **An upload failure never fails the build**; it is reported in the job
  summary, so Dependency-Track being down doesn't stop deploys. A one-off backfill uploads the attested SBOMs
  of every digest currently pinned in this repo.
- **Telemetry.** Metrics are on (management port 9000, `/metrics`), scraped by mon; dashboard and alerts as
  code (ADR 0027): feed mirror older than 48 h, and a new critical in a latest project. New critical and high
  findings on latest versions also go to Slack from Dependency-Track's own notifications.
- **Scope of v1** is images built here. Not in v1: SBOMs for third-party images, matching projects to the
  digests running in each cluster, licence and component policies, forwarding to DefectDojo (step 5a).

#### Considered options

1. **Dependency-Track on platform with platform-db (chosen).**
2. **Dependency-Track with its own Postgres** (a second StatefulSet or the chart's quickstart database): one
   more database to size, back up and restore, for a service whose data can be rebuilt from the SBOMs in Zot.
3. **Dependency-Track 4.x** (chart 1.x): still patched, but 5.x is the line that gets new features, and a 4 to
   5 move later is a reinstall with a data migration; starting on 5 avoids it.
4. **GUAC** or **Trivy's server mode**: GUAC is a graph of supply-chain facts with no triage workflow or
   dashboards; Trivy server rescans an image on request but keeps no inventory and doesn't re-check old SBOMs
   on its own.
5. **Only Trivy in CI** (today): finds CVEs at build time and never again.

#### Consequences

- Good: one place shows every component and licence in the images built here, and new advisories are matched
  against them daily without a rebuild.
- Good: the SBOMs ADR 0033 already produces get a reader; no new scanner in CI.
- Good: sign-in and roles come from the same Keycloak groups as every other app.
- Bad: about 2 to 3 GiB of memory on `platform` and one more database in platform-db; its dumps grow with the
  inventory.
- Bad: a KEK to keep. If `kv/platform/dependency-track kek` is lost, the stored feed tokens and webhooks must
  be entered again.
- Bad: a CVE that only NVD knows (not yet in the distro's data) isn't matched to an OS package. The distros
  publish within days, and NVD matching of OS packages was mostly noise here.
- Bad: "latest" means the newest `main` build, which Argo CD runs, but not proof of what runs in each cluster
  until digest matching is added.

#### Related

- [ADR 0032](#adr-0032): Trivy at build time; this adds the inventory after it.
- [ADR 0033](#adr-0033): the SBOM attestations this reads.
- [ADR 0035](#adr-0035): one database and owner per service.
- [ADR 0072](#adr-0072): third-party charts, values in the Application.
- [ADR 0078](#adr-0078): app groups as the only place access is assigned.
- [ADR 0027](#adr-0027): the dashboard and alerts.

## ADR-0100: The shared module repository is renamed platform-as-code, and its checkout folder keeps its name
{: #adr-0100 }

<span class="adr-status adr-status-proposed">Proposed</span> <span class="adr-meta text-muted">2026-11-12 · `platform-as-code`, `infra-aws`, `infra-azure`, `infra-cloudflare`, `infra-gcp`, `infra-github`, `infra-local`, `platform-wardens`, `reusable-workflows`</span>

#### Context

ADR 0098 named the framework PaC - Platform as Code and kept the repository name `terragrunt-shared-module` for later.
The name appears in two different ways:

- the **GitHub repository** `cloudutsukpilot/terragrunt-shared-module`: every consumer's `terragrunt-cicd.yaml` calls
  its reusable workflows with `uses: <org>/<repo>/.github/workflows/*.yaml@main` (rendered from `iac_shared_repo` in
  the consumer's `config.yaml`), Stack Author reads module docs from it, and the standards catalogue lists it;
- the **checkout folder** `terragrunt_shared_module`: about 420 Terragrunt units find it with
  `find_in_parent_folders("terragrunt_shared_module")`, and it is in `src/`, the CI checkout paths, every consumer's
  `entrypoint.py` and the shared NFS path on the Spark and the homelab PC.

GitHub redirects git clone, fetch and push and the web UI after a rename, but not `uses:` references to a renamed
repository's workflows: those fail with `repository not found`. A new repository with the old name ends the redirects.

#### Decision

- Rename the GitHub repository to `cloudutsukpilot/platform-as-code`.
- Keep the checkout folder `terragrunt_shared_module`. Nobody sees it, and changing it touches every unit and every
  machine's path for no change in behaviour.
- Prepare every pull request first (shared module, the six consumers, platform-wardens, reusable-workflows), rename,
  then merge them straight away. Never create a new repository called `terragrunt-shared-module`.

#### Considered options

1. **Repository only** (chosen): about ten small pull requests, consumer CI down only between the rename and the
   consumer merges.
2. **Repository and folder**: one name everywhere, but a change to about 420 units, the CI paths, every consumer's
   `entrypoint.py`, `.gitignore`, `.yamllint` and `Taskfile.yml`, and both machines' paths, while other sessions work
   in the same checkout. No infrastructure change either way (Terraform only re-downloads module sources).
3. **New repository, archive the old one** (GitHub's advice for renamed actions): no redirect gap, but it loses the
   pull request history and the read token's and GitHub App's repository grants.
4. **Keep the name**: no work, but the repository keeps a name the framework outgrew.

#### Consequences

- Good: the repository has the framework's name; nothing changes for Terraform state (keys are named after the
  consumers), the read token or the GitHub App (both bound to the repository ID).
- Good: the terraform-drift agent reads `iac_shared_repo` from each consumer, so it follows the consumers' change.
- Bad: consumer CI fails with `repository not found` from the rename until each consumer's pull request merges.
- Bad: two names to explain: the repository `platform-as-code`, the folder `terragrunt_shared_module`.

#### Related

- ADR 0098
- Shared module plan: `docs/plans/rename-to-platform-as-code.md` (phase 2)

## ADR-0101: Locust load tests run as CronJobs in perf on platform, report through OTLP and alert in Slack
{: #adr-0101 }

<span class="adr-status adr-status-proposed">Proposed</span> <span class="adr-meta text-muted">2026-11-14 · `homelab-gitops`, `homelab-services`</span>

#### Context

ADR 0031 chose Locust for nightly load tests in a `perf` namespace, with thresholds that fail the run and
results on a Performance dashboard. ADR 0020's SLO targets stay `proposed` until a first Locust baseline exists.
Neither says where the generator runs, how results reach `mon`, what a failed run does, or which of the
platform's ~40 apps are worth loading. Phase 3a step 4 builds it now, ahead of the rest of Phase 3a (owner
decision, 2026-11-14): nothing in it needs Phase 3. The plan is
docs/plans/locust-load-testing.md.

#### Decision

- **Where:** two CronJobs in namespace `perf` on `platform`: `perf-nightly` (22:30 UTC, after the Velero
  nightly and the 21:xx jobs) and `perf-weekly-gpu` (Sunday 23:30, after the nightly). On demand:
  `kubectl create job --from=cronjob/<name>`. `concurrencyPolicy: Forbid`, `backoffLimit: 0`,
  `activeDeadlineSeconds`, generator limits; the pod passes every Kyverno policy. One Locust process per
  scenario (no master and workers): at most 50 users, which gevent handles in one process.
- **Code:** `homelab-services/services/loadtest`, a `uv` workspace member with one locustfile per scenario and a
  `thresholds.yaml`, built by `build-image.yaml` like any service (amd64, signed, digest in `apps/perf`).
  Locust is pinned at 2.46.6: 2.47.0 is newer than the workspace's `exclude-newer` house rule allows.
- **Targets:** nightly: hello-world (harness calibration), LiteLLM with `mock_response` (gateway overhead, no
  GPU), embeddings, Qdrant search on `perf-*` collections, the portal's catalog and search APIs. Weekly GPU:
  chat time to first token, then gemma (never together, the model arbiter would swap them), then image-gen at
  1 to 2 users. On demand only: Keycloak's token endpoint, job-hunter, shorts-studio, lab-runner read endpoints.
  `rag-api` joins when Phase 5 builds it.
- **Never loaded:** anything through Cloudflare; `mon`'s stores; SearXNG and `mcp-web-search` (public search
  engines behind them); Hermes (personal data); databases, Zot, OpenBao, PowerDNS, Temporal (other tools if
  ever needed); `video-gen`; admin UIs and agent workers. Model-level throughput stays with `vllm bench serve`.
- **Measurement:** step-load shapes to find the knee; GPU scenarios warm the model first and record cold start
  apart from the p95; time to first token is its own request event from the streamed response.
- **Results:** OpenTelemetry metrics (`--otel`, traces and logs off) through the cluster's collector to Mimir:
  the harness's own histogram `loadtest_request_duration_seconds_*{job="perf/<scenario>", instance="<run id>",
  request_type, name, failed}`, next to Locust's own metrics. Locust's `locust.client.duration` can't carry the
  dashboard: the SDK's default buckets put every request under 5 s in one bucket, and it has no request type, so
  time to first token merges with the full answer (found on the first run, 2026-11-14). Dashboard "Load tests" in
  the Grafana **Performance** folder. No Locust traces to Tempo.
- **A failed run:** the threshold check sets a non-zero exit code, the Job fails, and a Mimir rule
  `LoadTestFailed` on kube-state-metrics alerts in **Slack** through Alertmanager. No GitHub issue (owner
  decision, 2026-11-14): no GitHub token in the cluster for it.
- **Phoenix:** `perf` calls go through LiteLLM with their own virtual key (`perf`, rpm limit, budget, tag
  `perf`, `turn_off_message_logging`). LiteLLM's Phoenix callback stays on for them, so the gateway's
  overhead is measured as it is in real use; the OTLP gateway drops their spans in `traces/phoenix`, so
  Phoenix and `ai-db` hold only real traffic. Tempo keeps them. The exact span attribute to filter on is
  checked against a real `perf` span when the filter is written.
- **Credentials** in OpenBao under `kv/platform/perf/*`: the LiteLLM key, a Backstage static token (catalog
  and search only), a Keycloak client `perf`, Qdrant access confined to `perf-*`.

- Amended (2026-11-14, owner request): **a web UI as well**, for trying scenarios by hand. A Deployment
  `locust-ui` in `perf` runs `loadtest ui` (Locust's web UI, every scenario in its class picker) at
  `https://locust.platform.home.cloudutsuk.com`, internal only, behind an oauth2-proxy sidecar and the Keycloak
  client `locust`, gated to `platform-admins` and `locust-admin` (ADR 0078), like the wardens console. Still no
  Locust master or operator: the UI is one process, and the scheduled runs stay headless CronJobs, which own the
  thresholds and the alert. UI runs report as `job="perf/ui"` and set no exit code; only the picked scenario's
  warm-up and setup run. A UI run sends real load, so it is not started while a scheduled one is going.

#### Considered options

1. **Scheduled GitHub Actions on the ARC runners**: an HTML report artifact and an issue come for free, but a
   30-minute run holds a CI runner, and it moves away from ADR 0031's `perf` namespace.
2. **Locust operator or Helm chart with a standing master and web UI**: a UI nobody watches at 04:00, and one
   more controller to keep up. A headless Job is enough at homelab scale. (The amendment adds a single-process
   UI for runs by hand, without a master or operator.)
3. **CronJob in `perf`, OTLP metrics, Slack alert** (chosen).
4. **Phoenix: keep `perf` spans**: shows load next to real traffic, but tens of thousands of synthetic spans a
   night bury real traces and fill `ai-db`; Grafana already shows the load metrics.

#### Consequences

- Good: ADR 0020's targets get data; regressions show up the morning after.
- Good: Uses the platform's own paths (build, Kyverno, OTLP, Alertmanager); nothing new to operate.
- Bad: The generator shares the homelab PC with `platform` and `ai`; the dashboard shows host CPU next to
  latency so a starved generator isn't mistaken for a slow target.
- Bad: A failure is a Slack message, not a tracked issue; follow-up is by hand.
- Bad (amendment): one more signed-in UI to keep up (Keycloak client, two secrets, a Homarr tile), and an admin
  can start load by hand at any time; the gate keeps that to admins.

#### Related

- [ADR 0020](#adr-0020)
- [ADR 0031](#adr-0031)
- [ADR 0063](#adr-0063)
- [ADR 0093](#adr-0093)

{% endraw %}
<!-- adr:end -->
